        <script id="save-data-connection-testing">
            function hasConnection() {
                return navigator.connection || navigator.mozConnection || navigator.webkitConnection || navigator.msConnection;
            }

            function createLink(src) {
                var preloadLink = document.createElement("link");
                preloadLink.rel = "preload";
                preloadLink.href = src;
                preloadLink.as = "font";
                preloadLink.type = "font/woff2";
                preloadLink.crossOrigin = "";
                document.head.insertBefore(preloadLink, document.head.firstChild);
            }

            var connectionDetail = {
                saveDataEnabled: false,
                slowConnection: false
            };

            var connection = hasConnection();
            if (connection) {
                connectionDetail.saveDataEnabled = connection.saveData;
                if (/\slow-2g|2g/.test(connection.effectiveType)) {
                    connectionDetail.slowConnection = true;
                }
            }

            if (!(connectionDetail.saveDataEnabled || connectionDetail.slowConnection)) {
                createLink("/oscar-static/fonts/HardingText-Regular-Web-cecd90984f.woff2");
            } else {
                document.documentElement.classList.add('save-data');
            }
        </script>

    (function () {
        if ( typeof window.CustomEvent === "function" ) return false;
        function CustomEvent ( event, params ) {
            params = params || { bubbles: false, cancelable: false, detail: null };
            var evt = document.createEvent( 'CustomEvent' );
            evt.initCustomEvent( event, params.bubbles, params.cancelable, params.detail );
            return evt;
        }

        CustomEvent.prototype = window.Event.prototype;

        window.CustomEvent = CustomEvent;
    })();
</script>

            if (h === 'preview-www.nature.com') return;
            var e = d.createElement(t),
                s = d.getElementsByTagName(t)[0];
            e.setAttribute('crossorigin', 'anonymous');
            if (h === 'nature.com' || h.endsWith('.nature.com')) {
                e.src = 'https://cmp.nature.com/production_live/en/consent-bundle-8-latest.js';
                
                    e.setAttribute('onload', "initGTM(window,document,'script','dataLayer','GTM-MRVXSHQ')");
                
            } else {
                e.src = '/oscar-static/js/cookie-consent-es5-bundle-c7e13f01d7.js';
                e.setAttribute('data-consent', h);
            }
            s.insertAdjacentElement('afterend', e);
        }
        cc();
    })(window,document,'script');
</script>

    <script type="application/ld+json">{"mainEntity":{"headline":"Inhibition of epithelial cell YAP-TEAD/LOX signaling attenuates pulmonary fibrosis in preclinical models","description":"Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) is a progressive and lethal disease characterized by excessive extracellular matrix deposition. Current IPF therapies slow disease progression but do not stop or reverse it. The (myo)fibroblasts are thought to be the main cellular contributors to excessive extracellular matrix production in IPF. Here we show that fibrotic alveolar type II cells regulate production and crosslinking of extracellular matrix via the co-transcriptional activator YAP. YAP leads to increased expression of Lysl oxidase (LOX) and subsequent LOX-mediated crosslinking by fibrotic alveolar type II cells. Pharmacological YAP inhibition via verteporfin reverses fibrotic alveolar type II cell reprogramming and LOX expression in experimental lung fibrosis in vivo and in human fibrotic tissue ex vivo. We thus identify YAP-TEAD/LOX inhibition in alveolar type II cells as a promising potential therapy for IPF patients. Extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling is a hallmark of fibrosis thought to be driven by mesenchymal cells. Here, the authors discover that YAP-TEAD/LOX axis is activated in distal lung epithelial cells, which contributes to ECM remodeling in pre-clinical models of pulmonary     <meta name="dc.rightsAgent" content="journalpermissions@springernature.com"/>
    <meta name="dc.description" content="Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) is a progressive and lethal disease characterized by excessive extracellular matrix deposition. Current IPF therapies slow disease progression but do not stop or reverse it. The (myo)fibroblasts are thought to be the main cellular contributors to excessive extracellular matrix production in IPF. Here we show that fibrotic alveolar type II cells regulate production and crosslinking of extracellular matrix via the co-transcriptional activator YAP. YAP leads to increased expression of Lysl oxidase (LOX) and subsequent LOX-mediated crosslinking by fibrotic alveolar type II cells. Pharmacological YAP inhibition via verteporfin reverses fibrotic alveolar type II cell reprogramming and LOX expression in experimental lung fibrosis in vivo and in human fibrotic tissue ex vivo. We thus identify YAP-TEAD/LOX inhibition in alveolar type II cells as a promising potential therapy for IPF patients. Extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling is a hallmark of fibrosis thought to be driven by mesenchymal cells. Here, the authors discover that YAP-TEAD/LOX axis is activated in distal lung epithelial cells, which contributes to ECM remodeling in pre-clinical models of pulmonary fibrosis."/>

    <meta name="description" content="Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) is a progressive and lethal disease characterized by excessive extracellular matrix deposition. Current IPF therapies slow disease progression but do not stop or reverse it. The (myo)fibroblasts are thought to be the main cellular contributors to excessive extracellular matrix production in IPF. Here we show that fibrotic alveolar type II cells regulate production and crosslinking of extracellular matrix via the co-transcriptional activator YAP. YAP leads to increased expression of Lysl oxidase (LOX) and subsequent LOX-mediated crosslinking by fibrotic alveolar type II cells. Extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling is a hallmark of fibrosis thought to be driven by mesenchymal cells. Here, the authors discover that YAP-TEAD/LOX axis is activated in distal lung epithelial cells, which contributes to ECM remodeling in pre-clinical models of pulmonary fibrosis."/>

                <div class="c-article-body">
                    <section aria-labelledby="Abs1" data-title="Abstract" lang="en"><div class="c-article-section" id="Abs1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Abs1">Abstract</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Abs1-content"><p>Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) is a progressive and lethal disease characterized by excessive extracellular matrix deposition. Current IPF therapies slow disease progression but do not stop or reverse it. The (myo)fibroblasts are thought to be the main cellular contributors to excessive extracellular matrix production in IPF. Here we show that fibrotic alveolar type II cells regulate production and crosslinking of extracellular matrix via the co-transcriptional activator YAP. YAP leads to increased expression of Lysl oxidase (LOX) and subsequent LOX-mediated crosslinking by fibrotic alveolar type II cells. Pharmacological YAP inhibition via verteporfin reverses fibrotic alveolar type II cell reprogramming and LOX expression in experimental lung fibrosis in vivo and in human fibrotic tissue ex vivo. We thus identify YAP-TEAD/LOX inhibition in alveolar type II cells as a promising potential therapy for IPF patients.</p></div></div></section>

                        <div class="main-content">
                            <section data-title="Introduction"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec1">Introduction</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec1-content"><p>Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) is a fatal, chronic lung disease with unknown ethiology<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Wolters, P. J., Collard, H. R. &amp; Jones, K. D. Pathogenesis of idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Annu. Rev. Pathol. 9, 157–179 (2014)." href="#ref-CR1" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1311">1</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Wolters, P. J. et al. Time for a change: is idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis still idiopathic and only fibrotic?. Lancet Respir. Med. 6, 154–160 (2018)." href="#ref-CR2" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1311_1">2</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Martinez, F. J. et al. Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Rev. Dis. Prim. 3, 17074 (2017)." href="#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1311_2">3</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Martinez, F. J. &amp; Lederer, D. J. Focus on idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis: advancing approaches to diagnosis, prognosis, and treatment. Chest 154, 978–979 (2018)." href="#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1311_3">4</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 5" title="Noth, I. &amp; Martinez, F. J. Recent advances in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Chest 132, 637–650 (2007)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1314">5</a></sup>. IPF is characterized by excessive extracellular matrix (ECM) deposition in the lung parenchyma resulting in progressive loss of lung function<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 6" title="Fernandez, I. E. &amp; Eickelberg, O. New cellular and molecular mechanisms of lung injury and fibrosis in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Lancet 380, 680–688 (2012)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1318">6</a></sup>. At present, there is no cure and median survival following diagnosis is between 2 and 3 years, worse than many malignancies. Recent studies have shown that distal lung epithelial injury and subsequent aberrant reprogramming of alveolar epithelial type 2 (AT2) cells may play a more prominent pathological role than previously realized, with several studies demonstrating anti-fibrotic effects when targeting fibrotic AT2 cells<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Anathy, V. et al. Reducing protein oxidation reverses lung fibrosis. Nat. Med. 24, 1128–1135 (2018)." href="#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1322">7</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Adams, T. S. et al. Single-cell RNA-seq reveals ectopic and aberrant lung-resident cell populations in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Adv. 6, eaba1983 (2020)." href="#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1322_1">8</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Pullamsetti, S. S. et al. The role of dimethylarginine dimethylaminohydrolase in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Transl. Med. 3, 87ra53 (2011)." href="#ref-CR9" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1322_2">9</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Konigshoff, M. et al. WNT1-inducible signaling protein-1 mediates pulmonary fibrosis in mice and is upregulated in humans with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. J. Clin. Invest. 119, 772–787 (2009)." href="#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1322_3">10</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11" title="Yu, G. et al. Thyroid hormone inhibits lung fibrosis in mice by improving epithelial mitochondrial function. Nat. Med. 24, 39–49 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1325">11</a></sup>. Moreover, several recent large-scale efforts to dissect the cellular landscape of IPF tissue using single cell RNA sequencing and spatial transcriptomics<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Franzén, L. et al. Mapping spatially resolved transcriptomes in human and mouse pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Genet. 56, 1725–1736 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1329">12</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Mayr, C. H. et al. Spatial transcriptomic characterization of pathologic niches in IPF. Sci. Adv. 10, eadl5473 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1332">13</a></sup> have independently and collectively identified the appearance of diverse cell states in IPF, including populations of distal lung epithelial cells which are not detected in normal lungs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="Adams, T. S. et al. Single-cell RNA-seq reveals ectopic and aberrant lung-resident cell populations in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Adv. 6, eaba1983 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1336">8</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Xu, Y. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing identifies diverse roles of epithelial cells in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 1, e90558 (2016)." href="#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1339">14</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Heinzelmann, K. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing identifies G-protein coupled receptor 87 as a basal cell marker expressed in distal honeycomb cysts in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Eur. Respir. J. 59, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1183/13993003.02373-2021&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1339_1">15</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Habermann, A. C. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing reveals profibrotic roles of distinct epithelial and mesenchymal lineages in pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Adv. 6, eaba1972 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1342">16</a></sup>.</p><p>The nuclear effectors of the Hippo pathway and known mechano-transducers, YAP and TAZ have recently been found to be expressed by transient alveolar epithelial cell types that are abnormally accumulated in IPF<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="LaCanna, R. et al. Yap/Taz regulate alveolar regeneration and resolution of lung inflammation. J. Clin. Invest. 129, 2107–2122 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1349">17</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Elbediwy, A., Vincent-Mistiaen, Z. I. &amp; Thompson, B. J. YAP and TAZ in epithelial stem cells: a sensor for cell polarity, mechanical forces and tissue damage. Bioessays 38, 644–653 (2016)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1352">18</a></sup>. However, the mechanisms of how increased expression of YAP and/or TAZ in AT2 cells and their therapeutic potential for human IPF, remains unexplored. Here, we explore the consequences of YAP activation in fibrotic AT2 cells. We demonstrate that fibrotic AT2 cells exhibit increased YAP activity and actively contribute to one of the main features of the fibrotic lung, increased ECM deposition. YAP regulates the expression and secretion of the ECM modifying enzyme lysyl oxidase (LOX) in fibrotic AT2 cells, which represents a previously unknown mechanism for AT2 cells to contribute to pulmonary fibrosis. Using the FDA-approved drug verteporfin (VP) and human fibrotic lung tissue cultures, we demonstrate that this mechanism is amenable to therapeutic intervention.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Results"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec2-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec2">Results</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec2-content"><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec3">Increased YAP activity in the alveolar epithelium in IPF</h3><p>Recently published data from our group and others have identified increased YAP activity in the fibrotic alveolar epithelium<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 15" title="Heinzelmann, K. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing identifies G-protein coupled receptor 87 as a basal cell marker expressed in distal honeycomb cysts in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Eur. Respir. J. 59, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1183/13993003.02373-2021&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2022)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1368">15</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gokey, J. J., Patel, S. D. &amp; Kropski, J. A. The role of Hippo/YAP signaling in alveolar repair and pulmonary fibrosis. Front. Med.8, 752316 (2021)." href="#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1371">19</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. Active epithelial Hippo signaling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 3, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.98738&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1371_1">20</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Strunz, M. et al. Alveolar regeneration through a Krt8+ transitional stem cell state that persists in human lung fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 11, 3559 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1374">21</a></sup>. Our single cell RNA sequencing (scRNASeq) dataset revealed cell-type specific gene expression of YAP, TAZ, and YAP target genes in alveolar epithelial cells with <i>YAP</i> as the highest expressed transcriptional coregulator of the Hippo pathway in alveolar epithelial cells, and increased expression of the YAP target gene connective tissue growth factor (CTGF) in transitional/aberrant alveolar epithelial cells in IPF, further indicating YAP activity is increased in IPF (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1A</a>)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22" title="Neumark, N., Cosme, C. Jr, Rose, K. A. &amp; Kaminski, N. The idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis cell atlas. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 319, L887–l893 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR22" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1384">22</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Schupp, J. C. et al. Integrated single-cell atlas of endothelial cells of the human lung. Circulation 144, 286–302 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1387">23</a></sup>. We performed immunohistochemistry of control and IPF tissue and, confirming the scRNASeq data, found increased active, nuclear YAP expression predominantly in alveolar epithelial cells associated with regions of visible epithelial remodeling, while TAZ expression was found mainly in mesenchymal cells in fibrosis (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1B</a> and Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S1A</a>) which matches other previous reports which focused on TAZ activity in fibroblasts<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Liu, F. et al. Mechanosignaling through YAP and TAZ drives fibroblast activation and fibrosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 308, L344–357 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1398">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25" title="Noguchi, S. et al. TAZ contributes to pulmonary fibrosis by activating profibrotic functions of lung fibroblasts. Sci. Rep. 7, 42595 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR25" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1401">25</a></sup>. Active YAP was found largely in aberrant distal lung epithelial cells marked by proSP-C positivity, a marker of AT2 cells, HOPX, marking transitional or AT1 cells, and KRT7, a marker of simple distal epithelial cells which is also highly elevated in aberrant basaloid populations. These cells were present in regions which were histologically abnormal (e.g. honeycombing regions), indicating either epithelial reprogramming of distal cells or migration of proximal cells into the distal region following injury (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1B</a> and Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S1A</a>). Importantly, we also observed aberrant YAP activation in regions of IPF lungs with only moderate levels of histological injury, indicating that changes in YAP may be an early event in IPF development (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1B</a> and Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S1</a>). In further support of this finding, we also found that nuclear YAP/TAZ is subjectively increased in the ‘normal looking’ regions of fibrotic murine lungs, including in alveolar type II cells marked by DC-Lamp (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1C</a>). Importantly, these regions have not yet undergone extensive remodeling and still have reported stiffness values in the range of normal lung tissue<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Liu, F. et al. Mechanosignaling through YAP and TAZ drives fibroblast activation and fibrosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 308, L344–357 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1421">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26" title="Tschumperlin, D. J., Ligresti, G., Hilscher, M. B. &amp; Shah, V. H. Mechanosensing and fibrosis. J. Clin. Invest 128, 74–84 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1424">26</a></sup>. In addition to mechanical stimuli such as stretch and stiffness, YAP is known to be regulated by the Hippo pathway, which is known to be active under conditions of high cell density regulating YAP cytosolic <i>versus</i> nuclear location. Thus, we examined Hippo signaling components (Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S1</a>) in the Lung Genome Research Consortium dataset, which contains whole lung homogenates of &gt;100 patients with IPF. Notably, we found that Hippo pathway component gene expression was able to separate patients with IPF from 91 normal patients using PCA (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1D</a>), however, no differences were observed in Hippo pathway components between normal patients and those with COPD (<i>n </i>= 144), indicating that loss of Hippo pathway components is not a universal feature of chronic lung diseases (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1D</a>). Moreover, decreased expression of Hippo pathway components and increased expression of fibrotic markers (<i>MMP7</i> and <i>MUC16</i>) correlated with lung function (as assessed by %DLCO) and with patients with more severe disease (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1E</a>). Together, these data support the hypothesis that YAP signaling is activated early in aberrant fibrotic AT2 in lung fibrosis, however, the functional impact of these findings and the potential consequences for therapeutic intervention, remains unknown.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-1" data-title="YAP/TAZ is upregulated and active in the aberrant epithelium in IPF."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig1" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 1: YAP/TAZ is upregulated and active in the aberrant epithelium in IPF.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div aberrant epithelium in IPF." loading="lazy" width="685" height="807"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-1-desc"><p><b>A</b> Publicly available scRNA-Seq data showing cell type specific gene expression in distal (alveolar) epithelial cells of IPF and Donor (Ctrl) lungs (IPFcellatlas.com: GSE135893). Changes in expression of Hippo family members, YAP, TAZ, and target gene CTGF in Donor <i>versus</i> IPF fibrotic epithelial cell subtypes is shown. (Tr. Transitional). <b>B</b> Representative Immunohistochemical staining of YAP, TAZ, epithelial, and mesenchymal markers on tissue sections for Donor tissue, moderate-fibrotic IPF, and full fibrotic IPF tissues. <i>n</i> = 6 donor, <i>n</i> = 6 moderate-fibrotic IPF, <i>n</i> = 13 full fibrotic IPF. <b>C</b> Representative immunofluorescence staining of Yap/Taz and Dc-Lamp, a marker of alveolar type II cells, on paraffin sections of murine lungs from PBS and bleomycin treated mice after 14 days. Scalebar 20 um on the top row. Bottom row is a digital zoom 5x. <i>n</i> = 5 PBS, <i>n</i> = 8 Bleomycin. <b>D</b> Principal component analysis (PCA) of microarray analysis on samples from control subjects (<i>n</i> = 91), IPF (<i>n</i> = 100), and COPD (<i>n</i> = 144) patients from the Lung Genome Research Consortium (LGRC) dataset using Hippo pathway genes found in Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">1</a>. <b>E</b> Pearson correlation coefficient (r<sup>2</sup>) between %DLCO measurements from Donor/IPF patients from the LGRC and gene expression of upstream Hippo signaling components <i>STK4, LATS2, WWC1, WWC3</i> and the profibrotic markers <i>MMP7</i> and <i>MUC16</i>. <i>p</i> &lt; 0.05 considered significant; two-tailed. No adjustment made for multiple comparisons (<i>k</i> = 6 comparisons). Source data for Panels (<b>A</b>, <b>D</b>, and <b>E</b>) are provided as a Source Data file.</p></div></div></figure></div><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec4">YAP-TEAD inhibition attenuates lung fibrosis in vivo</h3><p>There are limited therapeutic options for IPF. Recent studies have started to investigate the involvement of YAP/TAZ in lung fibrosis in vivo. While a combined YAP/TAZ deletion in AT2 cells led to exaggerated lung fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="DiGiovanni, G. T. et al. Epithelial Yap/Taz are required for functional alveolar regeneration following acute lung injury. JCI Insight 8, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.173374&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1554">27</a></sup>, we recently demonstrated that genetic loss-of-function of YAP (with preserved TAZ function) in AT2 cells led to attenuated pulmonary fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Warren, R., Lyu, H., Klinkhammer, K. &amp; De Langhe, S. P. Hippo signaling impairs alveolar epithelial regeneration in pulmonary fibrosis. Elife 12, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.85092&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1558">28</a></sup>. In order to further gain insight into the in vivo function of YAP signaling, and to further extend studies to potential pharmacological treatment approaches for IPF, we next sought to determine whether pharmacological intervention with a known suppressor of the YAP-TEAD transcriptional complex and FDA approved compound, verteporfin (VP), could reduce fibrotic burden in vivo<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 29" title="Puerta Cavanzo, N. et al. Verteporfin ameliorates fibrotic aspects of Dupuytren’s disease nodular fibroblasts irrespective the activation state of the cells. Sci. Rep. 12, 13940 (2022)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR29" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1562">29</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Shi-Wen, X. et al. Verteporfin inhibits the persistent fibrotic phenotype of lesional scleroderma dermal fibroblasts. J. Cell Commun. Signal 15, 71–80 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1565">30</a></sup>. To this end, we used the well-established model of bleomycin-induced pulmonary fibrosis via intratracheal administration<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 31" title="Lehmann, M. et al. Senolytic drugs target alveolar epithelial cell function and attenuate experimental lung fibrosis ex vivo. Eur. Respir. J. 50, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1183/13993003.02367-2016&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR31" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1569">31</a></sup>. After lung injury was allowed to develop over the course of 7 days in vivo, a timepoint with well-established histological changes, we began administration of VP i.p. every other day until animals were euthanized on day 14 (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S2</a>). We observed increased survival of animals which received bleomycin and were therapeutically treated with VP as compared to those animals who received vehicle only (survival: PBS-veh (10/10), PBS-VP (10/10), Bleo-veh (10/16), Bleo-VP (15/16)) (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2A</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S2B</a>). Furthermore, we observed loss of nuclear Yap with VP treatment (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2B</a>) and a significant decrease in <i>Ctgf</i>, a known downstream target of YAP-TEAD signaling (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2C</a>). This was accompanied by a reduction of well-known fibrotic markers, including <i>Acta</i>2, <i>Serpine1</i>, and <i>Wisp1</i> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2C</a>). Animals treated with bleomycin and subjected to VP administration exhibited a reduction in cellular infiltrates and tissue density, as assessed histologically and quantified using the Modified Ashcroft Score (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2D, E</a>). In support of these findings, total collagen content of the lungs of animals who received bleomycin and were treated with VP was significantly decreased as compared to animals who received bleomycin only (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig2">2F</a>). Interestingly, these animals exhibited loss of collagen despite the fact that VP treatment did not decrease the elevated transcriptional levels of major fibrillar lung collagens in bleomycin treated animals, <i>Col1a1</i> and <i>Col3a1</i> (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S3</a>), indicating a potential post-translational mechanism of altered collagen deposition.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-2" data-title="Pharmacological inhibition of YAP-TEAD improves survival and attenuates experimental lung fibrosis in vivo."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig2" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 2: Pharmacological inhibition of YAP-TEAD improves survival and attenuates experimental lung fibrosis in vivo.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture survival and attenuates experimental lung fibrosis in vivo." loading="lazy" width="685" height="802"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x/figures/2" data-track-dest="link:Figure2 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 2" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-2-desc"><p><b>A</b> Survival analysis during 14 days of mice treated with PBS and Bleomycin (2U/kg) with or without verteporfin (VP) injections administered intraperitoneally every 48 h starting from day 7 to day 14 (45 mg/kg). *<i>p</i> &lt; 0.05 considered significant: log-rank (Mantel-Cox) test. <b>B</b> Representative immunofluorescence staining of Yap/Taz in tissue sections from normal and fibrotic mouse lungs injected with verteporfin (VP) in vivo, <i>n</i> = 10 in each PBS group and <i>n</i> = 16 in each Bleo group from two independent sets of experiments. Scalebar 100 μm (Inlay 3X). <b>C</b> Gene expression of <i>Ctgf</i>, <i>Acta2, Serpine1</i>, and <i>Wisp1</i> relative to <i>Hprt</i> of tissue homogenates from study described in 2a. Each dot represents an individual mouse who survived until day 14 of the study. Mean ± s.d. *<i>p</i> &lt; 0.05 assessed by one-way ANOVA with Holm-Šídák’s multiple comparisons test. <i>Ctgf</i> (PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.3422; Bleo-veh vs. Bleo-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.0134; PBS-veh vs. Bleo-veh, <i>p</i> = 0.0002); <i>Acta2</i> (PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.2482; Bleo-veh vs. Bleo-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.0180; PBS-veh vs. Bleo-veh, <i>p</i> = &lt;0.0001); <i>Serpine1</i> (PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.1054; Bleo-veh vs. Bleo-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.1054; PBS-veh vs. Bleo-veh, <i>p</i> = &lt;0.0001); <i>Wisp1</i> (PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.5661; Bleo-veh vs. Bleo-VP, <i>p</i> = 0.0836; PBS-veh vs. Bleo-veh, <i>p</i> = &lt;0.0001); <b>D</b> Representative Masson’s trichrome staining of paraffin sections of PBS and Bleomycin mouse lungs treated with VP, scale bar: 2 mm. All animals were stained thus <i>n</i> = 10 for both PBS groups and <i>n</i> = 10 for Bleomycin and <i>n</i> = 15 for Bleomycin-VP. <b>E</b> Modified Ashcroft Score of the histological staining of the mouse lungs described in (<b>A</b>), each dot represents the median score of 3 independent experts who performed blinded analysis on digitized histological slides containing multiple sections per animal. Thus, each dot represents the histological score per animal. Due to ordinal data, median with 95% confidence interval shown. *<i>q</i> &lt; 0.05 considered significant and assessed by Kruskal–Wallis, corrected for multiple comparisons using the two-stage linear step-up procedure of Benjamini, Krieger and Yekutieli (<i>q</i> value). PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP (<i>q</i> = 0.2969), PBS-Veh vs. Bleo-Veh (<i>q</i> = &lt;0.0001), Bleo-Veh vs. Bleo-VP (<i>q</i> = 0.028). <b>F</b> Collagen amount as measured by high performance liquid chromatography of total tissue homogenates of mouse lungs described in (<b>A</b>); Each dot represents an individual mouse who survived until day 14 of the study. Mean ± s.d. *<i>p</i> &lt; 0.05 assessed by one-way ANOVA with Holm-Šídák’s multiple comparisons test. PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP (<i>p</i> = 0.4841), PBS-Veh vs. Bleo-Veh (<i>p</i> = &lt;0.0001), Bleo-Veh vs. Bleo-VP (<i>p</i> = 0.0189) Source data for Panels (<b>A</b>, <b>C</b>, <b>E</b> and <b>F</b>) are provided as a Source Data file. Source Data for Panel (<b>D</b>) is deposited at S-BIAD1520.</p></div></div></figure></div><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec5">YAP signaling induces LOX expression and secretion by AT2 cells</h3><p>To further identify the potential mechanisms involved in YAP-driven profibrotic changes in the distal epithelium, we investigated primary normal and fibrotic AT2 cells isolated from murine lungs. Despite culture on stiff glass coverslips, nuclear expression of YAP/TAZ was significantly increased in fibrotic AT2 cells, while YAP/TAZ was found mainly in the cytosol of normal AT2 cells (Figs. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3A, B</a>). In order to explore the role of YAP/TAZ in fibrotic alveolar cells, we silenced YAP/TAZ (siYT) in primary and normal fibrotic AT2 cells and performed transcriptome analysis (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S4A–G</a>). In support of successful transcriptional knockdown, YAP/TAZ were among the most significantly downregulated genes in both normal and fibrotic AT2 cells, which we also confirmed on the gene and protein level along with reduction of <i>Ccn2(Ctgf)</i>, a well-known target of Yap/Taz (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S4A–C</a>). Next, we explored differentially regulated genes following Yap/Taz knockdown which are elevated in fibrotic AT2 cells and reduced with siYT. We found that pathways associated with ECM and collagen assembly were among the most significantly enriched pathways, including collagen and lysl oxidase family members (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3D</a>, Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">2</a>).</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-3" data-title="YAP/TAZ in fibrotic AT2 cells regulate LOX secretion and collagen crosslinking."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig3" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 3: YAP/TAZ in fibrotic AT2 cells regulate LOX secretion and collagen crosslinking.</b></figcaption><div Created in BioRender. Wagner, D. Right 3 columns show fold change expression in fibrotic AT2 cells subjected to siYT normalized with scramble control (sc). <b>E</b> Gene expression of lysl oxidase (<i>Lox</i>) in pmAT2 cells isolated from normal and fibrotic mice and culture with siYT, <i>n </i>= 3 independent isolations. Source data for Panels (<b>B</b>, <b>E</b> and <b>F</b>) are provided as a Source Data file. Source Data for Panel (<b>D</b>) is deposited E-MTAB-14643 and (<b>H</b>) is deposited at S-BIAD1520.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>Fibrillar collagen assembly is regulated through a series of intracellular and extracellular processing steps, including collagen modifying and crosslinking enzymes. Notably, upon YAP/TAZ knockdown, only <i>Lox</i> was significantly reduced among the LOX family members in our transcriptome dataset (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3D, E</a>). Therefore, we confirmed LOX gene and protein expression in fibrotic AT2 cells and its reduction upon YAP/TAZ silencing, indicating that YAP/TAZ-driven LOX expression in fibrotic AT2 cells might contribute to the ECM niche in fibrosis (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3D, E</a>). LOX is a well-known ECM modifying enzyme which has been previously shown to be elevated in murine models of lung fibrosis as well as in IPF patient samples<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 32" title="Aumiller, V. et al. Comparative analysis of lysyl oxidase (like) family members in pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Rep. 7, 149 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR32" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1948">32</a></sup>. It is critical for the extracellular assembly of the collagen triple-helix and deposition of both collagen and elastin in the lung. While LOX expression has previously been mainly associated with fibroblasts<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Vallet, S. D. &amp; Ricard-Blum, S. Lysyl oxidases: from enzyme activity to extracellular matrix cross-links. Essays Biochem 63, 349–364 (2019)." href="#ref-CR33" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1952">33</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Philp, C. J. et al. Extracellular matrix cross-linking enhances fibroblast growth and protects against matrix proteolysis in lung fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 58, 594–603 (2018)." href="#ref-CR34" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1952_1">34</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Chen, L., Li, S. &amp; Li, W. LOX/LOXL in pulmonary fibrosis: potential therapeutic targets. J. Drug Target 27, 790–796 (2019)." href="#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1952_2">35</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 36" title="Nguyen, X. X. et al. Lysyl oxidase directly contributes to extracellular matrix production and fibrosis in systemic sclerosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 320, L29–l40 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR36" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1955">36</a></sup> which are well-known to be major contributors to ECM deposition and turnover, a role for distal epithelial derived LOX has not yet been described. Interestingly, LOX was the most highly enriched LOX-family member in diverse distal epithelial cells identified in a recently published spatial transcriptomics dataset (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3G</a>) which minimizes cell biases which occur in single cell RNA seq technologies due to loss and/or preferential enrichment of certain cell types (e.g. over-representation of immune cells and underrepresentation of AT1 cells)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Mayr, C. H. et al. Spatial transcriptomic characterization of pathologic niches in IPF. Sci. Adv. 10, eadl5473 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1963">13</a></sup>. In particular, alveolar cells, including aberrant basaloid cells had the most elevated expression of LOX in the spatial transcriptomic dataset. Despite differences in the cell types reported by the different technological platforms, this is in line with recent scRNASeq datasets of IPF lungs that have identified a subset of fibrotic epithelial cells with deranged ECM component expression<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Habermann, A. C. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing reveals profibrotic roles of distinct epithelial and mesenchymal lineages in pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Adv. 6, eaba1972 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e1967">16</a></sup>. We sought to confirm this finding on the protein level and therefore performed co-staining of LOX with YAP and HTII-280, a marker of alveolar type II cells in parallel sections of human control lung and IPF tissue sections. We found evidence of LOX expression in the lumens of honeycombing regions and LOX secretion adjacent to the apical surface of HTII-280+ cells with nuclear YAP. Taken together, these independent datasets confirm that LOX is elevated in AT2 cells in IPF (and/or aberrant epithelial cells which express HTII-280) with active YAP (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig3">3G, H</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S5</a>, <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S6</a>).</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec6">YAP-TEAD inhibition reduces collagen fibril formation by fibrotic AT2 cells</h3><p>We next sought to determine whether the anti-fibrotic effects observed by VP in vivo were mediated by LOX. We found that LOX gene and protein expression was indeed reduced in whole lung homogenates from fibrotic mice that were treated with VP (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig4">4</a>A, B), while <i>Loxl1</i> was not (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S7</a>). These results were further corroborated by a reduction of LOX expression in distal epithelium in situ (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig4">4C</a>). Treatment of AT2 cells with VP in vitro resulted in reduced Lox transcript and protein expression, further suggesting that AT2 cells are a major target for VP in vivo (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig4">4D, E</a>). Importantly, <i>Lox</i> knockdown in normal or fibrotic primary murine AT2 cells did not result in loss of the AT2 phenotype or induce senescence, indicating that targeting of epithelial <i>Lox</i> could be a promising strategy (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S8</a>). Altogether, these data demonstrate that LOX is a downstream target of YAP-TEAD signaling in fibrotic AT2 cells and indicate that the fibrotic alveolar epithelium via active YAP-TEAD signaling and LOX secretion into the extracellular environment may contribute to extracellular collagen network formation in pulmonary fibrosis.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-4" data-title="YAP/TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces Lox expression and ECM crosslinking in fibrotic AT2 cells."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig4" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 4: YAP/TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces Lox expression and ECM crosslinking in fibrotic AT2 cells.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41467-025-61795-x/MediaObjects/41467_2025_61795_Fig4_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-4-desc" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs41467-025-61795-x/MediaObjects/41467_2025_61795_Fig4_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 4: YAP/TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces Lox expression and ECM crosslinking in fibrotic AT2 cells." loading="lazy" width="685" height="707"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x/figures/4" data-track-dest="link:Figure4 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 4" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-4-desc"><p><b>A </b><i>Lox</i> gene expression (<i>n </i>= 10 each) (*adjusted <i>p</i> &lt; 0.05 considered significant with one-way ANOVA with Holm-Šídák’s multiple comparisons test). Mean ± s.d. PBS-veh vs. PBS-VP (<i>p</i> = 0.5039), <i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001 for PBS-veh vs Bleo-veh, Bleo-veh vs. Bleo-VP (<i>p</i> = 0.400). <b>B</b> Immunoblotting of Lox protein on tissue homogenates of normal and fibrotic mouse lungs injected with VP in vivo, <i>n </i>= 3–4 as shown. <b>C</b> Representative immunofluorescence staining of Lox in tissue sections obtained from the same study described in (<b>A</b>) and (<b>B</b>) (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S2</a>), scale bar: 50 um. Equal protein was loaded into each well and confirmed through the use of stain-free gels which label tryptophans. Source data for Panels (<b>A</b>, <b>B</b>, <b>D</b> and <b>E</b>) are provided as a Source Data file.</p></div></div></figure></div><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec7">Fibrotic AT2 cells modify the ECM niche by altering fibrillar collagen assembly</h3><p>While AT2 cells are known to play a role in the formation of epithelial basement membrane formation mainly composed of laminins and network forming collagen IV, they have not been described to play a role in fibrillar collagen assembly (i.e. collagen I and III). In order to first determine whether secreted products from AT2 cells are sufficient to modify fibrillar collagen assembly, we established an assay whereby we incubated soluble telopeptide containing type I collagen in the presence of secretomes derived from normal and fibrotic AT2 cells, respectively (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig5">5A</a>). The telopeptide region of collagen at the N and C terminus has been previously shown to be the region in which LOX exerts its activity to stabilize the collagen triple helix via crosslinks; collagen type I is the major fibrillar collagen in the lung and most solid organs and is capable of self-assembly at 37 °C, including in the absence of cells. Interestingly, collagen networks formed in the presence of fibrotic AT2 cell secretomes were strikingly different in morphology as compared to normal AT2 cells and, in accordance with our in vivo data, supernatants from VP-treated fibrotic AT2 cells did not induce changes in collagen network formation as compared to the changes induced by fibrotic AT2 cell supernatants (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig5">5B</a> and Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S9</a>). Similar results were obtained from the secretome from fibrotic AT2 cells treated with siYT (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S10</a>). Notably, fibrotic broncheoalveolar lavage fluid (BALF), representative of the in vivo secretome, increased maximum collagen gelation significantly (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig5">5C, D</a>), and this effect was reduced upon treatment with beta-aminopropionitrile (BAPN), a known LOX inhibitor which has previously shown efficacy in vitro for lung fibroblast driven collagen assembly<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 37" title="Tjin, G. et al. Lysyl oxidases regulate fibrillar collagen remodelling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Dis. Model Mech. 10, 1301–1312 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR37" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2134">37</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 38" title="Kanzaki, M. et al. Controlled collagen crosslinking process in tissue-engineered fibroblast sheets for preventing scar contracture on the surface of lungs. J. Tissue Eng. Regen. Med. 7, 383–391 (2013)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR38" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2137">38</a></sup>. Reduced collagen crosslinking by LOX inhibition with BAPN was further confirmed by western blot under reducing conditions (i.e. to reduce disulfide bonds between proteins) with consistent loss of the β-band at 250 kDa, representing intermolecular crosslinking (i.e. collagen monomer dimerization). This mimics previous observations where BAPN was shown to exert its action through reduction of the β-band in tissue-engineered fibroblast sheets to generate collagen networks with reduced crosslinking through LOX inhibition<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 38" title="Kanzaki, M. et al. Controlled collagen crosslinking process in tissue-engineered fibroblast sheets for preventing scar contracture on the surface of lungs. J. Tissue Eng. Regen. Med. 7, 383–391 (2013)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR38" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2141">38</a></sup> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig5">5E</a>). Finally, we sought to assess collagen crosslinks present in lung tissue of mice which had been treated with verteporfin in vivo using Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy (FTIR) in ATR-Transmission mode. As expected, we observed significant upregulation of overall collagen content corresponding to mature collagen fibrils containing crosslinks at 1650 cm<sup>−1</sup> (Amide I) in PBS versus BLEO treated animals<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 39" title="Nashchekina, Y. A. et al. Molecular and fibrillar structure collagen analysis by FTIR spectroscopy. J. Phys. Conf. Ser. 1697, 012053 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR39" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2150">39</a></sup>. Interestingly, verteporfin caused a reduction in this peak as well as a decrease in the Amide II band at 1550 cm<sup>−1</sup>. These data strengthen a putative role for active YAP-TEAD/LOX in the fibrotic alveolar epithelium driving altered extracellular collagen network crosslinking and thus formation in pulmonary fibrosis (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig5">5F–H</a>, Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">3</a>).</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-5" data-title="AT2 cell derived LOX mediates collagen gelation."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig5" Source data for Panels (<b>C</b>–<b>H</b>) are provided as a Source Data file. Source Data for Panel (<b>B</b>) is deposited at S-BIAD1520.</p></div></div></figure></div><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec8">YAP-TEAD inhibition reduces fibrosis in human pulmonary tissue ex vivo</h3><p>Lastly, in order to explore whether targeting this identified YAP-TEAD/LOX axis is a potential therapy for patients with IPF, we examined the anti-fibrotic activity of VP in a highly complex ex vivo human 3D tissue culture model using precision cut-lung slices (PCLS). Previously, we have demonstrated that living human PCLS treated with a pro-fibrotic cocktail (TGFβ1, TNFα, PDGF-AB, and LPA) led to a robust fibrotic signature<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Alsafadi, H. N. et al. An ex vivo model to induce early fibrosis-like changes in human precision-cut lung slices. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 312, L896–l902 (2017)." href="#ref-CR40" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2282">40</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gerckens, M. et al. Generation of human 3D lung tissue cultures (3D-LTCs) for disease modeling. J. Vis. Exp. &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3791/58437&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2019)." href="#ref-CR41" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2282_1">41</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Lehmann, M. et al. Differential effects of Nintedanib and Pirfenidone on lung alveolar epithelial cell function in ex vivo murine and human lung tissue cultures of pulmonary fibrosis. Respir. Res. 19, 175 (2018)." href="#ref-CR42" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2282_2">42</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 43" title="Gerckens, M. et al. Phenotypic drug screening in a human fibrosis model identified a novel class of antifibrotic therapeutics. Sci. Adv. 7, eabb3673 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR43" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2285">43</a></sup>. This human ex vivo model for IPF can successfully mimic injury and early fibrosis-like responses in human lung tissue. As our initial data indicates that modification of the ECM niche via YAP-TEAD activation in distal epithelium may be an early driver of disease (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig1">1B</a> and Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S1</a>), this model provides a unique opportunity to explore whether YAP-TEAD inhibition targets LOX expression and fibrosis markers in human lung tissue ex vivo. To this end, we subjected hPCLS to single nuclei RNA Sequencing (snRNA-Seq) analysis to further gain insights into cell-specific effects of VP. We profiled 76259 nuclei from a total of 24 PCLS obtained from two independent donor lungs each subjected to Fibrotic Cocktail (FC) with/without VP or the respective DMSO control, as previously described<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Alsafadi, H. N. et al. An ex vivo model to induce early fibrosis-like changes in human precision-cut lung slices. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 312, L896–l902 (2017)." href="#ref-CR40" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2295">40</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gerckens, M. et al. Generation of human 3D lung tissue cultures (3D-LTCs) for disease modeling. J. Vis. Exp. &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3791/58437&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2019)." href="#ref-CR41" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2295_1">41</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Lehmann, M. et al. Differential effects of Nintedanib and Pirfenidone on lung alveolar epithelial cell function in ex vivo murine and human lung tissue cultures of pulmonary fibrosis. Respir. Res. 19, 175 (2018)." href="#ref-CR42" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2295_2">42</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 43" title="Gerckens, M. et al. Phenotypic drug screening in a human fibrosis model identified a novel class of antifibrotic therapeutics. Sci. Adv. 7, eabb3673 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR43" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2298">43</a></sup>. We identified all major expected cell lineages (epithelial, stromal, endothelial and immune) in our dataset (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S11</a>) and found that FC increased known YAP-TEAD target genes <i>CCN1</i> and <i>CCN2</i> in epithelial cells (and <i>CCN2</i> to a lesser extent in fibroblasts), which were both downregulated with VP treatment (Supplementary Figs. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S11</a>, <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">12</a>). We mainly focused on fibrotic epithelial cells for our further analysis due to our previous finding that YAP and LOX were elevated in the fibrotic distal epithelium. Five major epithelial cell types (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6A</a>) based on distinct markers with no discernible batch condition or cell cycle effects on data were identified (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6B</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S13</a>). Similarly to a recent hPCLS study utilizing scRNA-Seq data<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 44" title="Lang, N. J. et al. Ex vivo tissue perturbations coupled to single-cell RNA-seq reveal multilineage cell circuit dynamics in human lung fibrogenesis. Sci. Transl. Med. 15, eadh0908 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR44" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2331">44</a></sup>, FC stimulation induced the emergence of aberrant basaloid epithelial cells (<i>KRT17</i> + <i>KRT5−</i>) at day 5 which we were able to further delineate into two separate subclusters which we termed aberrant basaloid-like 1 (ABL1) (elevated levels of <i>FN1</i>, <i>TNC, TGFBI</i>, <i>SERPINE1</i>) and 2 (ABL2) (elevated levels of <i>AREG</i> and <i>MUC21</i>) (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6A–C</a>). Interestingly, ABL1 cells were enriched in genes associated with collagen matrix production (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6D</a>) and while some ABL1 cells were present in the unstimulated conditions, the majority of these cells arose following FC stimulation indicating a population induced by FC rather than culture conditions (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6E</a>). In addition, we identified a small cluster of basal cells (<i>TP63, KRT5, EYA2</i>) which only appeared under vehicle or FC + VP conditions but was noticeably absent from FC-only stimulated samples across patients (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6E</a>). On the other hand, FC conditions induced the presence of a subcluster we termed inflammatory basal cells due to their co-expression of basal cell markers (<i>TP63</i>) and inflammatory markers previously associated with acute and chronically injured human airway epithelium (<i>ICAM1</i><sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 45" title="Tosi, M. F. et al. Induction of ICAM-1 expression on human airway epithelial cells by inflammatory cytokines: effects on neutrophil-epithelial cell adhesion. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 7, 214–221 (1992)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR45" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2378">45</a></sup>). Noticeably FC induced <i>LOX</i>, and to a lesser extent <i>LOXL1</i> and <i>LOXL2</i>, in the aberrant basaloid populations (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6F–H</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S14</a>) and fibroblasts (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S15</a>) which was subsequently decreased following VP stimulation (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6F</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S15</a>). While VP stimulation, did not influence the epithelial cell type proportions (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6E</a>), it significantly affected the gene signature of aberrant basaloid cells, including downregulation of <i>YAP</i> and <i>TAZ</i> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6F, G</a>), as well as significant decreases of ECM, profibrotic and EMT genes such as <i>TNC, SERPINE1, MMP9</i>, and <i>CXCL12</i> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6G</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S16</a>). These data support that notion that fibrotic epithelial cells, such as the aberrant basaloid cells present in IPF lungs, produce <i>LOX</i> and that VP treatment affects this cell type and induces anti-fibrotic effects.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-6" data-title="YAP-TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces profibrotic genes in human fibrotic lung tissue ex vivo."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig6" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 6: YAP-TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces profibrotic genes in human fibrotic lung tissue ex vivo.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div Source data for Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig6">6</a> are provided at <a href="https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.14229565">https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.14229565</a>.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>The increase of LOX gene and protein expression upon fibrotic cocktail treatment and its reduction upon VP was further validated in an independent hPCLS cohort derived from 5 donor lungs upon fibrosis induction with FC (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig7">7B–D</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S17</a>). In addition, fibrotic marker gene expression was differentially reduced with VP treatment as compared to Pirfenidone and Nintedanib (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S18</a>). Finally, we further validated our results in PCLS from end-stage IPF tissue explants and found that treatment with VP reduced <i>LOX</i> expression as well as <i>CTGF</i>, <i>WISP1</i> and <i>FN1</i> in IPF tissue (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig7">7E, F</a>). Interestingly, VP treatment led to an increase in epithelial/AT2 cells marker <i>ECAD</i> and <i>SFTPC</i>, suggesting a beneficial effect of VP on epithelial cell function in IPF.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-7" data-title="YAP-TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces LOX and fibrotic markers in human fibrotic lung tissue ex vivo."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig7" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 7: YAP-TEAD inhibition by verteporfin reduces LOX and fibrotic markers in human fibrotic lung tissue ex vivo.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture Panels (<b>A</b> and <b>E</b>) Created in BioRender. Wagner, D. (2025) <a href="https://BioRender.com/nmwzx11">https://BioRender.com/nmwzx11</a>. Source data for Panels (<b>B</b>–<b>D</b>, <b>F</b>) are provided as a Source Data file.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>Overall, we report a mechanism on how fibrotic AT2 cells modify the ECM niche, including fibrillar collagen assembly, which is driven through a deranged YAP/TEAD-LOX axis. By modifying the ECM niche, AT2 cells might play a major role in fostering a self-perpetuating vicious cycle of YAP-TEAD/LOX activation driving fibrosis (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#Fig8">8</a>). Inhibition of this axis by VP led to attenuation of fibrosis in vivo as well as in human pulmonary fibrosis ex vivo thus representing a potential therapeutic avenue.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-8" data-title="Summary of proposed vicious Hippo-YAP/LOX mediated extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling cycle driven by lung epithelial cells in IPF."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig8" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 8: Summary of proposed vicious Hippo-YAP/LOX mediated extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling cycle driven by lung epithelial cells in IPF.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" mediated extracellular matrix (ECM) remodeling cycle driven by lung epithelial cells in IPF." loading="lazy" width="685" height="814"></picture><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x/figures/8" data-track-dest="link:Figure8 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 8" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-8-desc"><p>Derangement of the Hippo signaling pathway components lead to the activation of YAP-TEAD activity in the nucleus leading to increased gene expression of matrix molecules and modifying enzymes (such as LOX) leading to increased secretion of LOX from the fibrotic alveolar epithelial cells, which contributes to increased crosslinking of secreted matrix in the ECM. This increase in matrix crosslinking causes further changes in the mechanical stiffness that leads to further increase in YAP activity. Created in BioRender. Wagner, D. (2025) <a href="https://BioRender.com/nmwzx11">https://BioRender.com/nmwzx11</a>.</p></div></div></figure></div></div></div></section><section data-title="Discussion"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec9-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec9">Discussion</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec9-content"><p>Pulmonary fibrosis is a devastating progressive lung disease with limited therapeutic options. Here we identify aberrant YAP/TEAD/LOX signaling in fibrotic alveolar cells as a central mechanism in pulmonary fibrosis onset and progression. We demonstrate that fibrotic alveolar cells exhibit increased YAP activity and actively contribute to one of the main features of the fibrotic lung, increased ECM secretion, deposition and crosslinking. YAP regulates the expression and secretion of the ECM modifying enzyme lysyl oxidase (LOX) in fibrotic alveolar cells, which represents a previously unknown mechanism for AT2 cells to contribute to pulmonary fibrosis. Using the FDA-approved drug verteporfin and human fibrotic lung tissue cultures, we demonstrate that this mechanism is amenable to therapeutic intervention. We build upon and extent recent studies that highlight the diverse and dynamic roles of YAP/TAZ signaling in lung injury and (impaired) repair<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="LaCanna, R. et al. Yap/Taz regulate alveolar regeneration and resolution of lung inflammation. J. Clin. Invest. 129, 2107–2122 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2709">17</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gokey, J. J., Patel, S. D. &amp; Kropski, J. A. The role of Hippo/YAP signaling in alveolar repair and pulmonary fibrosis. Front. Med.8, 752316 (2021)." href="#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2712">19</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. Active epithelial Hippo signaling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 3, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.98738&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2712_1">20</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Strunz, M. et al. Alveolar regeneration through a Krt8+ transitional stem cell state that persists in human lung fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 11, 3559 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2715">21</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Liu, F. et al. Mechanosignaling through YAP and TAZ drives fibroblast activation and fibrosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol.
