Physiol. 308, L344–357 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2718">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Tschumperlin, D. J., Ligresti, G., Hilscher, M. B. &amp; Shah, V. H. Mechanosensing and fibrosis. J. Clin. Invest 128, 74–84 (2018)." href="#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2721">26</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="DiGiovanni, G. T. et al. Epithelial Yap/Taz are required for functional alveolar regeneration following acute lung injury. JCI Insight 8, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.173374&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2721_1">27</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Warren, R., Lyu, H., Klinkhammer, K. &amp; De Langhe, S. P. Hippo signaling impairs alveolar epithelial regeneration in pulmonary fibrosis. Elife 12, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.85092&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2724">28</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. YAP regulates alveolar epithelial cell differentiation and AGER via NFIB/KLF5/NKX2-1. iScience 24, 102967 (2021)." href="#ref-CR46" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2728">46</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Chen, Y. et al. YAP1/Twist promotes fibroblast activation and lung fibrosis that conferred by miR-15a loss in IPF. Cell Death Differ. 26, 1832–1844 (2019)." href="#ref-CR47" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2728_1">47</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Penkala, I. J. et al. Age-dependent alveolar epithelial plasticity orchestrates lung homeostasis and regeneration. Cell Stem Cell 28, 1775–1789.e1775 (2021)." href="#ref-CR48" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2728_2">48</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 49" title="Hicks-Berthet, J. et al. Yap/Taz inhibit goblet cell fate to maintain lung epithelial homeostasis. Cell Rep. 36, 109347 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR49" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2731">49</a></sup>. Early YAP/TAZ studies in the lung have largely focused and established its mechanotransduction role in pulmonary fibroblasts<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Liu, F. et al. Mechanosignaling through YAP and TAZ drives fibroblast activation and fibrosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 308, L344–357 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2735">24</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 47" title="Chen, Y. et al. YAP1/Twist promotes fibroblast activation and lung fibrosis that conferred by miR-15a loss in IPF. Cell Death Differ. 26, 1832–1844 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR47" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2738">47</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 50" title="Haak, A. J. et al. Selective YAP/TAZ inhibition in fibroblasts via dopamine receptor D1 agonism reverses fibrosis. Sci. Transl. Med. 11, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1126/scitranslmed.aau6296&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR50" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2741">50</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 51" title="Konkimalla, A. et al. Transitional cell states sculpt tissue topology during lung regeneration. Cell Stem Cell 30, 1486–1502.e1489 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR51" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2744">51</a></sup> however, the observation that YAP/TAZ are further activated in lung epithelial cells, has only emerged of late and the mechanistic and functional consequences of epithelial cell YAP/TAZ signaling represent an area of active investigations. A critical role for YAP/TAZ signaling in the lung epithelium in the context of pre- and postnatal lung development has been found, in which YAP has been shown to regulate cell differentiation including proximal airway differentiation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 52" title="Szymaniak, A. D., Mahoney, J. E., Cardoso, W. V. &amp; Varelas, X. Crumbs3-mediated polarity directs airway epithelial cell fate through the Hippo pathway effector yap. Dev. Cell 34, 283–296 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR52" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2748">52</a></sup>, YAP and TAZ in AT2 to AT1 differentiation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 46" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. YAP regulates alveolar epithelial cell differentiation and AGER via NFIB/KLF5/NKX2-1. iScience 24, 102967 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR46" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2752">46</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 48" title="Penkala, I. J. et al. Age-dependent alveolar epithelial plasticity orchestrates lung homeostasis and regeneration. Cell Stem Cell 28, 1775–1789.e1775 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR48" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2755">48</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 49" title="Hicks-Berthet, J. et al. Yap/Taz inhibit goblet cell fate to maintain lung epithelial homeostasis. Cell Rep. 36, 109347 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR49" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2758">49</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Zhao, R. et al. Yap tunes airway epithelial size and architecture by regulating the identity, maintenance, and self-renewal of stem cells. Dev. Cell 30, 151–165 (2014)." href="#ref-CR53" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2761">53</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Nantie, L. B. et al. Lats1/2 inactivation reveals Hippo function in alveolar type I cell differentiation during lung transition to air breathing. Development 145, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1242/dev.163105&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="#ref-CR54" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2761_1">54</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 55" title="Burgess, C. L. et al. Generation of human alveolar epithelial type I cells from pluripotent stem cells. Cell Stem Cell 31, 657–675.e658 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR55" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2764">55</a></sup> as well as bronchial morphogenesis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 56" title="Isago, H. et al. Epithelial expression of YAP and TAZ is sequentially required in lung development. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 62, 256–266 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR56" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2768">56</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 57" title="Mahoney, J. E., Mori, M., Szymaniak, A. D., Varelas, X. &amp; Cardoso, W. V. The Hippo pathway effector yap controls patterning and differentiation of airway epithelial progenitors. Dev. Cell 30, 137–150 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR57" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2771">57</a></sup> and sacculation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 58" title="van Soldt, B. J. et al. Yap and its subcellular localization have distinct compartment-specific roles in the developing lung. Development 146, dev175810 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR58" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2776">58</a></sup>. The functional contribution of YAP and/or TAZ in the context of lung fibrosis, which is characterized by dramatic epithelial cell reprogramming<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 59" title="Blackwell, T. S. et al. Future directions in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis research. An NHLBI workshop report. Am. J. Respir. Crit. Care Med. 189, 214–222 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR59" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2780">59</a></sup>, is less explored. Gokey et al. have reported increased YAP activity in bronchial epithelial cells in fibrosis, leading to mTOR/PI3K/AKT-driven cell proliferation and migration, supporting a profibrotic role for YAP in the airways<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. Active epithelial Hippo signaling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 3, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.98738&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2784">20</a></sup>. Similar findings have been reported by Stancil et al. in airway epithelial cells, identifying ERBB/YAP signaling as a potential driver of airway remodeling<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 60" title="Stancil, I. T. et al. Pulmonary fibrosis distal airway epithelia are dynamically and structurally dysfunctional. Nat. Commun. 12, 4566 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR60" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2788">60</a></sup>. We recently showed that Yap but not Taz lead to bronchiolization in lung parenchyma through cooperation with Myc<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 61" title="Warren, R. et al. Cell competition drives bronchiolization and pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 10624 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR61" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2792">61</a></sup>. These initial observations are extended in our studies, presenting several lines of evidence that fibrotic AT2 cell states in the human and mouse lung exhibit high levels of nuclear YAP. We demonstrate that fibrotic YAP-active AT2 cells contribute to collagen crosslinking and ECM deposition. We identify a previously unknown profibrotic mechanism, in which YAP expression does not only contribute to changes in the cellular phenotype of fibrotic AT2 cells but further drives the expression of the ECM crosslinking enzyme LOX leading to increased ECM/collagen formation and crosslinking in pulmonary fibrosis. YAP1 has been found to be enriched in the LOX promotor region in a cancer cell line, suggesting a direct effect of YAP1 on LOX expression<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 62" title="Chen, P. et al. Symbiotic macrophage-glioma cell interactions reveal synthetic lethality in PTEN-null glioma. Cancer Cell 35, 868–884.e866 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR62" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2796">62</a></sup>. To this end, we demonstrated that YAP inhibition, using either a pharmacological compound (VP) or siRNA-mediated knockdown, reduces LOX expression in AT2 cells in vitro, experimental lung fibrosis in vivo, and human lung fibrosis modeled in PCLS ex vivo.</p><p>YAP activation in fibrosis has been largely investigated in the context of its role as a mechanotransducer in mesenchymal cells, which is activated due to increased stiffness in fibrosis. However, recent work has also examined a role for activated YAP/TAZ in endothelial cells in the context of aging-associated fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 63" title="Raslan, A. A. et al. Lung injury-induced activated endothelial cell states persist in aging-associated progressive fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 5449 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR63" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2803">63</a></sup>. Furthermore, several recent publications have identified dysregulated Yap/Taz or TEAD signaling networks in epithelial cells through bioinformatic approaches<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Franzén, L. et al. Mapping spatially resolved transcriptomes in human and mouse pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Genet. 56, 1725–1736 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2807">12</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Strunz, M. et al. Alveolar regeneration through a Krt8+ transitional stem cell state that persists in human lung fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 11, 3559 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2810">21</a></sup> but did not follow up on these findings. We identified increased epithelial YAP/TAZ activity even in ‘normal looking’ regions of fibrotic lungs, in which little changes in stiffness have been described<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 64" title="White, E. S., Thomas, M., Stowasser, S. &amp; Tetzlaff, K. Challenges for clinical drug development in pulmonary fibrosis. Front Pharm. 13, 823085 (2022)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR64" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2814">64</a></sup>, suggesting a previously unknown role for the activation of YAP that is independent of ECM stiffness. YAP is regulated by the developmental Hippo pathway<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 65" title="Varelas, X. The Hippo pathway effectors TAZ and YAP in development, homeostasis and disease. Development 141, 1614–1626 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR65" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2818">65</a></sup> and we found that loss of Hippo pathway components in IPF correlated with YAP/TAZ activation, leading to the hypothesis that loss of this pathway in normal epithelium might be sufficient to drive distal epithelial cells towards aberrant cellular phenotypes observed in IPF. This is substantiated by previous studies, which have found dysregulated Hippo component transcripts in single cell RNAseq data sets<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 14" title="Xu, Y. et al. Single-cell RNA sequencing identifies diverse roles of epithelial cells in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 1, e90558 (2016)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2822">14</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Gokey, J. J. et al. Active epithelial Hippo signaling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. JCI Insight 3, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.98738&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2825">20</a></sup>. Accumulating evidence in the adult lung suggests a role of the Hippo components and upstream YAP regulators serine/threonine-specific kinases (STKs) Mst1 and Mst2 as well as Lats 1 and 2 in lung development and adult regeneration. Loss of Mst1 and 2 revealed distorted lung maturation with decreased alveolar sacculation and increased cellularity, features also seen in pulmonary fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 66" title="Lange, A. W. et al. Hippo/Yap signaling controls epithelial progenitor cell proliferation and differentiation in the embryonic and adult lung. J. Mol. Cell Biol. 7, 35–47 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR66" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2830">66</a></sup>. We recently showed that loss of Mst1/2 or Nf2, another upstream Hippo kinase, in AT2 cells stabilizes Taz, permitting its translocation into the nucleus to drive AT1 cell differentiation. Interestingly, loss of Taz, but not Yap, in AT2 cells inhibited differentiation to AT1 cells, indicating divergent roles of Yap and Taz in AT2 to AT1 differentiation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 61" title="Warren, R. et al. Cell competition drives bronchiolization and pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 10624 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR61" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2834">61</a></sup>. Loss of Lats1/2 in airway secretory cells (i.e. Scgb1a1) has been shown to induce Yap/Taz activation which was accompanied by loss of the secretory phenotype, acquisition of transitional markers and evidence of subepithelial fibrosis, including activation of fibroblasts and collagen deposition<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 67" title="Jeon, H. Y. et al. Airway secretory cell fate conversion via YAP-mTORC1-dependent essential amino acid metabolism.  EMBO J. 41, e109365 (2022)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR67" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2838">67</a></sup>. Together, these studies point to cell and context dependent effects of Yap and Taz which are also regulated by loss of Hippo pathway components in the lung epithelium.</p><p>Increased ECM deposition is a consequence as well as a driver of pulmonary fibrosis, leading to a positive feedback loop<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 68" title="Herrera, J., Henke, C. A. &amp; Bitterman, P. B. Extracellular matrix as a driver of progressive fibrosis. J. Clin. Invest. 128, 45–53 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR68" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2845">68</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 69" title="Parker, M. W. et al. Fibrotic extracellular matrix activates a profibrotic positive feedback loop. J. Clin. Invest. 124, 1622–1635 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR69" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2848">69</a></sup>. Several data suggest that changes in the ECM alone are sufficient to induce alteration of normal cell types towards pro-fibrotic phenotypes<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 69" title="Parker, M. W. et al. Fibrotic extracellular matrix activates a profibrotic positive feedback loop. J. Clin. Invest. 124, 1622–1635 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR69" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2852">69</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 70" title="van der Velden, J. L. et al. TGF-β1-induced deposition of provisional extracellular matrix by tracheal basal cells promotes epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition in a c-Jun NH(2)-terminal kinase-1-dependent manner. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 314, L984–l997 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR70" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2855">70</a></sup>. The ECM in the IPF lung is highly dynamic and far from a static environment with little remodeling, as initially thought. Multiple ECM components (termed the matrisome) are continuously secreted, deposited and degraded in the IPF lung, with an overall sum of net deposition further remodeled and stiffened by crosslinking enzymes of e.g. lysyl oxidase (LOX) family<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 71" title="Jones, M. G. et al. Nanoscale dysregulation of collagen structure-function disrupts mechano-homeostasis and mediates pulmonary fibrosis. eLife 7, e36354 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR71" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2859">71</a></sup>. ECM crosslinking is a key process that perpetuates the profibrotic niche in IPF. The LOX/LOXL proteins have been put forward as potential targets for the treatment of IPF<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 35" title="Chen, L., Li, S. &amp; Li, W. LOX/LOXL in pulmonary fibrosis: potential therapeutic targets. J. Drug Target 27, 790–796 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2863">35</a></sup>. Previous studies have shown that LOX and the four LOX-like proteins (LOXL1-4) play a key role in crosslinking of the ECM<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Vallet, S. D. &amp; Ricard-Blum, S. Lysyl oxidases: from enzyme activity to extracellular matrix cross-links. Essays Biochem 63, 349–364 (2019)." href="#ref-CR33" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2867">33</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Philp, C. J. et al. Extracellular matrix cross-linking enhances fibroblast growth and protects against matrix proteolysis in lung fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 58, 594–603 (2018)." href="#ref-CR34" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2867_1">34</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Chen, L., Li, S. &amp; Li, W. LOX/LOXL in pulmonary fibrosis: potential therapeutic targets. J. Drug Target 27, 790–796 (2019)." href="#ref-CR35" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2867_2">35</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Nguyen, X. X. et al. Lysyl oxidase directly contributes to extracellular matrix production and fibrosis in systemic sclerosis. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 320, L29–l40 (2021)." href="#ref-CR36" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2867_3">36</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 37" title="Tjin, G. et al. Lysyl oxidases regulate fibrillar collagen remodelling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Dis. Model Mech. 10, 1301–1312 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR37" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2870">37</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 72" title="Ma, H. Y. et al. LOXL4, but not LOXL2, is the critical determinant of pathological collagen cross-linking and fibrosis in the lung. Sci. Adv. 9, eadf0133 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR72" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2873">72</a></sup>. Inhibition of LOX using β-Aminoproprionitrile (BAPN) reduced experimental pulmonary fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 37" title="Tjin, G. et al. Lysyl oxidases regulate fibrillar collagen remodelling in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Dis. Model Mech. 10, 1301–1312 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR37" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2878">37</a></sup>. While LOX expression has previously been detected in fibroblasts, the role of LOX expression in fibrotic AT2 cells has not been investigated. Our observations are in line with Aumiller et al demonstrating that LOX family members are expressed by epithelial cells in pulmonary fibrosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 32" title="Aumiller, V. et al. Comparative analysis of lysyl oxidase (like) family members in pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Rep. 7, 149 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR32" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2882">32</a></sup>, with the highest expression of LOX and LOXL4. Interestingly, in contrast to LOXL2, LOX expression was not induced by TGF-β1 in bronchial epithelial cells. LOX expression has been shown to be regulated by TGF-β1 in fibrosis in other cell types, such as fibroblasts<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 73" title="Laczko, R. &amp; Csiszar, K. Lysyl Oxidase (LOX): functional contributions to signaling pathways. Biomolecules 10, 1093 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR73" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2886">73</a></sup>; our data indicate that LOX expression in distal epithelial cells might be driven by other upstream factors of YAP, such as the Hippo pathway. Interestingly, one of the original studies that identified TEADs as the main transcription factor family interacting with YAP identified LOX but not LOXL2 as a downstream target of YAP-TEAD interactions in breast epithelial cells<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 74" title="Zhao, B. et al. TEAD mediates YAP-dependent gene induction and growth control. Genes Dev. 22, 1962–1971 (2008)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR74" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2890">74</a></sup>. A clinical trial targeting LOXL2 using the monoclonal antibody simtuzumab, found no benefit regarding survival<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 75" title="Raghu, G. et al. Efficacy of simtuzumab versus placebo in patients with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis: a randomised, double-blind, controlled, phase 2 trial. Lancet Respir. Med. 5, 22–32 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR75" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2894">75</a></sup>, although limited data about its bioavailability and target engagement in IPF are known. In our current study, we targeted the distal YAP-TEAD LOX axis using multiple distinct approaches which together strongly supports a role for distal epithelial-derived LOX in altered collagen assembly in the parenchyma in pulmonary fibrosis. First, we utilized BAPN to inhibit LOX in a real-time collagen assembly assay utilizing BALF from a murine model of pulmonary fibrosis. BAPN inhibits intra- and intermolecular covalent cross-linking of collagen and elastin connective tissue proteins<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 76" title="Barry-Hamilton, V. et al. Allosteric inhibition of lysyl oxidase-like-2 impedes the development of a pathologic microenvironment. Nat. Med. 16, 1009–1017 (2010)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR76" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2898">76</a></sup>. We found that LOX inhibition by BAPN reduced real-time collagen assembly induced by fibrotic BALF. While BAPN is described as a potent and irreversible LOX inhibitor, it has also affinity for other LOX family members and for other amine oxidases<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 77" title="Hajdú, I. et al. Inhibition of the LOX enzyme family members with old and new ligands. Selectivity analysis revisited. Bioorg. Med. Chem. Lett. 28, 3113–3118 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR77" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2903">77</a></sup>: thus, we cannot exclude off target effects as potential co-mediators of the effects we see due to the complex composition of BALF. However, we observed similar alterations in collagen assembly using supernatants from fibrotic alveolar cells with YAP/TAZ knockdown as well as those treated with verteporfin (VP). Therefore, our data strongly points to a mechanism by which altered YAP-TEAD activity induces LOX expression in distal epithelial cells which in turn alters local collagen assembly.</p><p>In order to show proof of principle for therapeutic inhibition of YAP-TEAD signaling via verteporfin, an FDA-approved drug for the treatment of macular degeneration, we utilized in vitro, in vivo and ex vivo assays utilizing human tissue. VP led to a reduction in LOX expression across all experimental conditions and furthermore reduced overall fibrosis severity in fibrotic AT2 cells in vitro, as well as in murine lungs in vivo and human fibrotic PCLS ex vivo. Importantly, VP treatment reduced the total amount of collagen, including the amount of crosslinked fibrillar collagen as assessed by FTIR. Thus, we identify a potential therapeutic approach, which targets cellular reprogramming of alveolar cells to aberrantly express LOX and regulate ECM deposition. Therapeutically targeting epithelial cells is an emerging approach in IPF<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 78" title="Ptasinski, V. A., Stegmayr, J., Belvisi, M. G., Wagner, D. E. &amp; Murray, L. A. Targeting alveolar repair in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 65, 347–365 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR78" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2910">78</a></sup>, and our data further strengthen this rationale by providing a previously underrecognized contribution of these cells to ECM deposition and remodeling. All together, we hypothesize that temporal dysregulation of Hippo components leads to YAP activation in AT2 cells in early stages of IPF which initiates alveolar reprogramming and onset of remodeling, while stiffness is an additional contribution in later stages of fibrosis.</p><p>We used a pharmacological approach to therapeutically intervene with YAP-TEAD signaling and demonstrated that the FDA approved drug verteporfin exhibited inhibitory effect of pro-fibrotic epithelial cells functions. While using this approach corroborates the potential translatability of our study, pharmacological approaches also exhibit limitations. We cannot exclude that VP treatment in vivo and ex vivo also affects other cell types; in fact, VP treatment on YAP/TAZ activated fibroblasts may be beneficial and we did observe evidence of inhibition of pro-fibrotic markers in fibroblasts in our snRNAseq dataset. Future studies should examine this in more detail as well as characterize other ECM deposition versus degradation pathways. Nonetheless, our snRNA-seq data and in vitro gelation assays supports the fact that VP indeed targets epithelial cells. snRNA-Seq analysis of VP-treated human fibrotic tissue revealed a strong effect of VP on the phenotype of aberrant basaloid cells and their LOX expression, which represent the dominant fibrotic epithelial cells in the fibrotic lung<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="Adams, T. S. et al. Single-cell RNA-seq reveals ectopic and aberrant lung-resident cell populations in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Sci. Adv. 6, eaba1983 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2918">8</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 79" title="Verheyden, J. M. &amp; Sun, X. A transitional stem cell state in the lung. Nat. Cell Biol. 22, 1025–1026 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR79" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2921">79</a></sup>. Recent single cell data from the hPCLS model predicted that these cells are derived from AT2 cells<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 44" title="Lang, N. J. et al. Ex vivo tissue perturbations coupled to single-cell RNA-seq reveal multilineage cell circuit dynamics in human lung fibrogenesis. Sci. Transl. Med. 15, eadh0908 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR44" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2925">44</a></sup>. While this in silico based hypothesis is further corroborated by recent organoid data from us<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 80" title="Ptasinski, V. et al. Modeling fibrotic alveolar transitional cells with pluripotent stem cell-derived alveolar organoids. Life Sci. Alliance 6, e202201853 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR80" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2929">80</a></sup> and others<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 81" title="Kathiriya, J. J. et al. Human alveolar type 2 epithelium transdifferentiates into metaplastic KRT5(+) basal cells. Nat. Cell Biol. 24, 10–23 (2022)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR81" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2933">81</a></sup> that FC treatment or mesenchymally-derived factors induce differentiation of human AT2 cells or iAT2 cells into aberrant basaloid cells (KRT17+, KRT5−), respectively, lineage tracing studies are needed to further test these assumptions.</p><p>Moreover, we recently reported that genetic loss-of-function of YAP, but not TAZ, in AT2 cells (driven by SPC) led to attenuated pulmonary fibrosis in vivo<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Warren, R., Lyu, H., Klinkhammer, K. &amp; De Langhe, S. P. Hippo signaling impairs alveolar epithelial regeneration in pulmonary fibrosis. Elife 12, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.85092&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2940">28</a></sup>, thus corroborating our findings. Of note, a recent study using combined YAP/TAZ deletion in AT2 cells demonstrated increased inflammation and failed repair in two different models of alveolar injury<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="DiGiovanni, G. T. et al. Epithelial Yap/Taz are required for functional alveolar regeneration following acute lung injury. JCI Insight 8, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1172/jci.insight.173374&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2944">27</a></sup>. Similarly, we recently showed that combined loss of Yap and Taz in bronchial epithelial cells (Sox2+) resulted in decreased AT1 cell regeneration and increased pulmonary fibrosis following bleomycin injury<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 61" title="Warren, R. et al. Cell competition drives bronchiolization and pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 10624 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR61" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2948">61</a></sup>. However, we found that Yap and Taz have divergent roles in epithelial repair. Active Taz in AT2 cells regulates AT1 cell differentiation while active Yap regulates basal-like cell mediated bronchiolization through cooperation with increased Myc levels<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 61" title="Warren, R. et al. Cell competition drives bronchiolization and pulmonary fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 10624 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR61" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2952">61</a></sup>. Interestingly, YAP deletion activates TAZ signaling, which can contribute to repair in vivo. These studies highlight the importance of furthering our understanding of the separate roles of YAP <i>versus</i> TAZ signaling as most data in the literature is based on effects elicit by combined YAP and TAZ.</p><p>YAP and TAZ have independent roles as co-transcription factors and are known to interact with a variety of transcription factors<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 82" title="Reggiani, F., Gobbi, G., Ciarrocchi, A. &amp; Sancisi, V. YAP and TAZ are not identical twins. Trends Biochem. Sci. 46, 154–168 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR82" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2962">82</a></sup>. It is incompletely understood which combinations of Yap and/or Taz transcription factor complexes promote regeneration versus which promote further injury or arrested regeneration. VP binds to TEAD and thus might inhibit both YAP-TEAD as well as TAZ-TEAD interactions. However, VP has been primarily reported to act on YAP interactions, based on affinity and protein expression levels in a given cell<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Bierbaumer, L. et al. YAP/TAZ inhibition reduces metastatic potential of Ewing sarcoma cells. Oncogenesis 10, 2 (2021)." href="#ref-CR83" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2966">83</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Dey, A., Varelas, X. &amp; Guan, K. L. Targeting the Hippo pathway in cancer, fibrosis, wound healing and regenerative medicine. Nat. Rev. Drug Discov. 19, 480–494 (2020)." href="#ref-CR84" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2966_1">84</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Elisi, G. M. et al. Repurposing of Drugs Targeting YAP-TEAD Functions. Cancers 10, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3390/cancers10090329&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2018)." href="#ref-CR85" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2966_2">85</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 86" title="Dong, L., Lin, F., Wu, W., Liu, Y. &amp; Huang, W. Verteporfin inhibits YAP-induced bladder cancer cell growth and invasion via Hippo signaling pathway. Int. J. Med. Sci. 15, 645–652 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR86" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2969">86</a></sup>. Thus, loss of Yap-TEAD interactions may promote Taz mediated regeneration. Notably, previous data and our scRNA-Seq as well as snRNA-Seq show that AT2 cells and basaloid cells have a high abundance of YAP expression, with fewer cells expressing TAZ and also at lower levels. This indicates YAP-TEAD inhibition as a prominent mechanism; however, the number of overall TAZ positive fibrotic cells was low, and future studies are needed to further investigate whether this is a direct effect or secondary to YAP-TEAD inhibition. Furthermore, YAP and TAZ have been shown to be regulated by, as well as capable of regulating, diverse developmental pathways which are known to be reactivated in IPF. This includes but is not limited to Sonic hedgehog signaling<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 56" title="Isago, H. et al. Epithelial expression of YAP and TAZ is sequentially required in lung development. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 62, 256–266 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR56" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2973">56</a></sup>, Wnt<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10" title="Konigshoff, M. et al. WNT1-inducible signaling protein-1 mediates pulmonary fibrosis in mice and is upregulated in humans with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. J. Clin. Invest. 119, 772–787 (2009)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2977">10</a></sup> and TGF-beta<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 52" title="Szymaniak, A. D., Mahoney, J. E., Cardoso, W. V. &amp; Varelas, X. Crumbs3-mediated polarity directs airway epithelial cell fate through the Hippo pathway effector yap. Dev. Cell 34, 283–296 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR52" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2981">52</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 57" title="Mahoney, J. E., Mori, M., Szymaniak, A. D., Varelas, X. &amp; Cardoso, W. V. The Hippo pathway effector yap controls patterning and differentiation of airway epithelial progenitors. Dev. Cell 30, 137–150 (2014)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR57" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2984">57</a></sup>. Future work should explore the complex interplay of epithelial YAP and TAZ with these pathways in the context of pulmonary fibrosis.</p><p>Our data support YAP-TEAD inhibition by VP as a potential therapeutic option for IPF. In support of this, YAP-TEAD inhibition by VP has been shown to be beneficial in liver and cardiac fibrosis as well as in skin fibroblast of systemic sclerosis<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Szeto, S. G. et al. YAP/TAZ are mechanoregulators of TGF-β-Smad signaling and renal fibrogenesis. J. Am. Soc. Nephrol. 27, 3117–3128 (2016)." href="#ref-CR87" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2991">87</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Francisco, J. et al. Blockade of fibroblast YAP attenuates cardiac fibrosis and dysfunction through MRTF-A inhibition. JACC Basic Transl. Sci. 5, 931–945 (2020)." href="#ref-CR88" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2991_1">88</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 89" title="Ma, F. et al. Systems-based identification of the Hippo pathway for promoting fibrotic mesenchymal differentiation in systemic sclerosis. Nat. Commun. 15, 210 (2024)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR89" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2994">89</a></sup>. Our data indicate that YAP activation in fibrosis might occur early in the disease and contributes to ECM deposition and stiffness, which in turn activates YAP signaling, thus developing a potential vicious feed-forward loop<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 90" title="Rackow, A. R. et al. The self-fulfilling prophecy of pulmonary fibrosis: a selective inspection of pathological signalling loops. Eur. Respir. J. 56, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.1183/13993003.00075-2020&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR90" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e2998">90</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 91" title="Freeberg, M. A. T. et al. Mechanical feed-forward loops contribute to idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Pathol. 191, 18–25 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR91" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3001">91</a></sup>. Targeting YAP also opens the potential opportunity to interfere early and stop and slow disease progression, however, further studies are needed to understand the temporal regulation and activity of YAP in disease. It is worth mentioning that other YAP inhibitors should be explored and that Metformin, which has been shown to elicit antifibrotic effects in the lung<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 92" title="Kheirollahi, V. et al. Metformin induces lipogenic differentiation in myofibroblasts to reverse lung fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 10, 2987 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR92" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3005">92</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 93" title="Rangarajan, S. et al. Metformin reverses established lung fibrosis in a bleomycin model. Nat. Med 24, 1121–1127 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR93" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3008">93</a></sup> has been reported to target YAP activity<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 94" title="Ye, L. et al. The inhibition of YAP signaling prevents chronic biliary fibrosis in the Abcb4(-/-) model by modulation of hepatic stellate cell and bile duct epithelium cell pathophysiology. Aging Dis, &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.14336/ad.2023.0602&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2023)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR94" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3012">94</a></sup>.</p><p>In conclusion, we identify the YAP-TEAD/LOX axis as a epithelial cell-driven pro-fibrotic mechanism, resulting in phenotypic cellular changes as well as ECM alterations, as a pharmacological approach to treat tissue fibrosis.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Methods"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec10-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec10">Methods</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec10-content"><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec11">Study design</h3><p>The aim of the study was to assess and investigate how fibrotic AT2 cells contribute to pulmonary fibrosis and specifically analyze the role of increased YAP activity in fibrotic AT2 cells. We used paraffin-embedded tissue from experimental lung fibrosis and human IPF and donor tissue from patients to assess YAP expression in situ. Human lung tissue samples were obtained from the CPC-M bioArchive at the Comprehensive Pneumology Center (CPC Munich, Germany) and the Tissue Repository at the University of Pittsburgh. Mice were randomly allocated into experimental groups prior to the onset of any experimentation. For experimental lung fibrosis, mice were subjected to a single dose of bleomycin (Sigma B5507) administered intratracheally via a microsprayer at 2U/kg body weight. The use of animals was approved under the ethics of the Helmholtz-Zentrum Munich and the state of Bavaria, Germany; project number: 55.2-1-54-2532-88-12. Reporting of animal experiments conforms to ARRIVE 2.0 guidelines. Mice were housed under 12-h light/12-h dark cycles at room temperature (20–24 °C) and humidity (45–65%). Animals were given free access to water and standard chow. 12-week-old female C57BL/6 mice were used for all animal experiments as indicated. Animals were weighed every day to monitor their health; animals which lost more than 15% body weight from the study onset were euthanized.</p><p>We isolated primary mouse AT2 cells from fibrotic (14 days after in vivo bleomycin application) and healthy lungs (14 days after in vivo PBS application) as outlined in detail below and subjected them to in vitro cultures, including siRNA-mediated loss-of-function studies according to published protocols<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Strunz, M. et al. Alveolar regeneration through a Krt8+ transitional stem cell state that persists in human lung fibrosis. Nat. Commun. 11, 3559 (2020)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3034">21</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Costa, R. et al. A drug screen with approved compounds identifies amlexanox as a novel Wnt/β-catenin activator inducing lung epithelial organoid formation. Br. J. Pharm. 178, 4026–4041 (2021)." href="#ref-CR95" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3037">95</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Lehmann, M. et al. Chronic WNT/β-catenin signaling induces cellular senescence in lung epithelial cells. Cell Signal 70, 109588 (2020)." href="#ref-CR96" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3037_1">96</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 97" title="Ulke, H. M. et al. The oncogene ECT2 contributes to a hyperplastic, proliferative lung epithelial cell phenotype in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 61, 713–726 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR97" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3040">97</a></sup>. Supernatants from AT2 cell cultures and BALF samples were subjected to an in vitro collagen fibril formation and gelation assay to investigate whether soluble factors, such as LOX, contribute to ECM crosslinking and deposition.</p><p>We further aimed to investigate the potential therapeutic effects of YAP inhibition in vivo and ex vivo. To this end, we used verteporfin as a known YAP-TEAD inhibitor in different in vivo and ex vivo models. For experimental lung fibrosis in vivo, we subjected mice to the well-known bleomycin model and used a therapeutic treatment regime (treatment from day 7 to day 14 after bleomycin). Fibrosis was assessed using qualitative and quantitative gold-standard readouts using a variety of molecular biological assays<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 98" title="Jenkins, R. G. et al. An official American Thoracic Society Workshop report: use of animal models for the preclinical assessment of potential therapies for pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 56, 667–679 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR98" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3047">98</a></sup>, as outlined below.</p><p>Fresh lung tissue of explanted Donor or IPF lungs was used for human PCLS according to previous published protocols<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 40" title="Alsafadi, H. N. et al. An ex vivo model to induce early fibrosis-like changes in human precision-cut lung slices. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 312, L896–l902 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR40" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3054">40</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 41" title="Gerckens, M. et al. Generation of human 3D lung tissue cultures (3D-LTCs) for disease modeling. J. Vis. Exp. &#xA;                  https://doi.org/10.3791/58437&#xA;                  &#xA;                 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR41" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3057">41</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 99" title="Liu, G. et al. Use of precision cut lung slices as a translational model for the study of lung biology. Respir. Res. 20, 162 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR99" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3060">99</a></sup>. Donor lung samples were obtained from the Center for Organ Recovery and Education (CORE) at the University of Pittsburgh. Donor lung samples originated from lungs deemed unsuitable for organ transplantation. For the fibrosis induction in hPCLS, PCLS were treated for 5 days with a control cocktail (CC) including all vehicles or a pro-fibrotic cocktail (FC) consisting of TGF-β (5 ng/ml, Bio-Techne), PDGF-AB (10 ng/ml, Thermo Fisher), TNF-α (10 ng/ml, Bio-Techne), and LPA (5 µM, Cayman chemical)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 40" title="Alsafadi, H. N. et al. An ex vivo model to induce early fibrosis-like changes in human precision-cut lung slices. Am. J. Physiol. Lung Cell Mol. Physiol. 312, L896–l902 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR40" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3064">40</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 42" title="Lehmann, M. et al. Differential effects of Nintedanib and Pirfenidone on lung alveolar epithelial cell function in ex vivo murine and human lung tissue cultures of pulmonary fibrosis. Respir. Res. 19, 175 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR42" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3067">42</a></sup>. For verteporfin treatments, hPCLS were treated with FC or CC to allow for induction of fibrosis and Verteporfin treatment started at day 3 until day 5. At the end of the experiment, PCLS were snap-frozen individually in liquid nitrogen for RNA or protein extractions as well as single nuclei analysis, as described below. The study was approved by the local ethics committee of the Ludwig-Maximilians University of Munich, Germany (Ethic vote #19-630) and University of Pittsburgh (IRB PRO14010265). Written informed consent was obtained for all study participants.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec12">Immunohistochemistry (IHC) and immunofluorescence (IF)</h3><p>Human lung tissue was placed in 4% (w/v) paraformaldehyde after explantation (fixation was done for 12–24 h) and processed for paraffin embedding. Sections (3 μm) were cut and mounted on positively charged glass slides (Super Frost Plus, Langenbrinck (Emmendingen, Germany)). Paraffin-embedded tissue sections of normal donor and IPF lungs were deparaffinized in xylene and rehydrated in graded alcohol. Antigens were retrieved by cooking the sections for 5 min in 10 mmol/L citrate buffer (pH 6.0) using microwave irradiation (800 W). Thereafter, sections cooled down for 20 min at RT, followed by repeated cooking (800 W, 5 min) and cooling (20 min at RT). This procedure was performed three times. Importantly, the citrate buffer was freshly prepared by mixing 18 mL 100 mmol/L citric acid monohydrate and 82 mL 100 mmol/L sodium citrate tribasic dihydrate with 900 mL distilled water. For immunostaining, the streptavidin-biotin-alkaline phosphatase (AP) method with use of the ZytoChem-Plus AP Kit (Fast Red) [Zytomed Systems, Berlin, Germany], according to the manufacturer´s protocol, was employed.</p><p>In general, sections were incubated for 2 h at RT with primary antibodies, which were diluted in PBS containing 2% (w/v) BSA. Control sections were treated with PBS-2%BSA alone to determine the specificity of the staining. Detection was performed with a polyvalent secondary biotinylated antibody (rabbit, mouse, rat, guinea pig, provided by the ZytoChem- Plus AP Kit, 20 min incubation) followed by incubation with AP-conjugated streptavidin (20 min). Sections were then developed with Fast Red substrate solution, and the reaction was terminated by washing in distilled water. The stained sections were counterstained with hemalaun (Mayers hemalaun solution, WALDECK Division CHROMA GmbH &amp; CO KG, Münster, Germany) and mounted in Glycergel (DakoCytomation). Lung tissue sections were scanned with a scanning device (Nano-Zoomer, Hamamatsu), and examined histopathologically using the ´NDP.view2 software´ at 100×, 200×, 400× and 800× original magnification.</p><p>Immunofluorescence of human lung histological sections was performed similarly as above except for the usage of secondary antibodies conjugated to fluorophores. Whole slides were scanned with an Olympus IX83 P2ZF using a 20× UPLXAPO objective with an NA of 0.8 and images were exported using the Olympus OlyVia 3.1 Software. Immunofluorescence of murine lung sections was performed as above and imaged with a Nikon A1+ Confocal. All primary and secondary antibodies and their concentrations are included in Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">6</a>.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec13">Histology of murine lungs</h3><p>Following ligation and surgical resection of the right lung lobes, left lung lobes were gravity-fixed via tracheal inflation with fixation solution (6% Paraformaldehyde (PFA)) for 10 min at room temperature followed by submersion in fixative over night at 4 °C. Individual lobes were then cut transversally into four sections and subjected to standard tissue processing and paraffin embedding. 5um sections were prepared using a microtome (Zeiss) and mounted on glass microscope slides. Hematoxylin and Eosin (HE) and Masson’s trichrome (MTC) staining were performed using standard protocols and then scanned using a MIRAX Scan (Zeiss) with Control Software v0.5.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec14">Modified Ashcroft scoring</h3><p>The fibrotic state of murine lungs was evaluated using the modified Ashcroft scoring<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 100" title="Hübner, R. H. et al. Standardized quantification of pulmonary fibrosis in histological samples. Biotechniques 44, 507–511 (2008). 514-507." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR100" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3104">100</a></sup>. Briefly, low and higher power magnification images were obtained from MTC stained and scanned murine lungs (MIRAX Viewer Software v.1.12.25.3). All samples were de-identified and randomized. Qualitative evaluation of the images was performed by three independent, blinded experts, scoring each image from a scale of 0–8 where 0 is normal lung to 8 being most fibrotic. Scores for each mouse were averaged across the three independent reviewers and samples were re-identified for further analysis.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec15">Collagen quantification</h3><p>Individual right lobes from each animal were used for analysis of hydroxyproline amounts by reverse-phase high performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) as a measure of collagen content. Amounts of hydroxyproline per lobe were correlated to total collagen amount using standard curves processed identically<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 101" title="Howell, D. C. et al. Absence of proteinase-activated receptor-1 signaling affords protection from bleomycin-induced lung inflammation and fibrosis. Am. J. Pathol. 166, 1353–1365 (2005)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR101" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3117">101</a></sup>.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec16">Primary alveolar epithelial type 2 (AT2) cell isolation</h3><p>12-week-old male C57BL/6 mice were administered either PBS or Bleomycin as described in the previous section and were sacrificed for cell isolation at day 14. AT2 cells were isolated as previously described using negative selection, resulting in 92–95% surfactant protein C-positive cells<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 102" title="Königshoff, M. et al. The angiotensin II receptor 2 is expressed and mediates angiotensin II signaling in lung fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 37, 640–650 (2007)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR102" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3129">102</a></sup>. Briefly, mice were anesthetized using ketamine-xylazine mixture and trachea was intubated with a modified 19 G needle (blunt end and grated edges). Bronchoalveolar lavage fluid (BALF) was collected twice with 500 ul PBS supplemented with protease inhibitors. Lungs were perfused with warm 0.9% NaCl through the heart and filled with 1.5 ml Dispase (50 Caseinolytic units/ml, BD Bioscience) and immediately capped with 300 ul 2% agarose and incubated for 45 min. Lungs from 4 mice were pooled for each isolation. Lung lobes were separated and minced using forceps and cells were passed through 100 um, 20 um and 10 um nylon filters. Cells were then plated in petri dishes and incubated at 37 °C for 30 min for negative selection of fibroblasts and macrophages. Unattached cells were collected and negative selection of CD45+/CD31+ cells was performed using MACs magnetic microbeads and separators. The remaining cells were plated in cell culture plates or on coverslips coated with Poly-L-lysine. Incubation duration and biological replicates are indicated in each experiment. DAEC complete medium consisted of high-glucose DMEM (D0822, Sigma) with 1% Pencillin/streptomycin, 1X glutaMax.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec17">AT2 cell culture, transfection and treatments</h3><p>Freshly isolated murine AT2 cells were seeded on tissue culture plastic and allowed to adhere and form epithelial monolayers for two days<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 103" title="Mutze, K., Vierkotten, S., Milosevic, J., Eickelberg, O. &amp; Königshoff, M. Enolase 1 (ENO1) and protein disulfide-isomerase associated 3 (PDIA3) regulate Wnt/β-catenin-driven trans-differentiation of murine alveolar epithelial cells. Dis. Model Mech. 8, 877–890 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR103" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3141">103</a></sup>. Cells were then transfected overnight with Lipofectamine RNAiMax (Invitrogen) according to the manufacturer’s instructions using 60 nM siRNA for Yap and Taz, (Yap, Taz from Santa Cruz: sc-38638, sc-38569), 30 nM for siLox (Integrated DNA Technologies, #488693007) and scrambled siRNA control (Santa Cruz or Origene; SR30004). Media was then changed to AT2 cell complete medium and harvested at 48 h after transfection. For verteporfin treatments, AT2 cells were freshly isolated and allowed to attach prior to treatment with 7 uM of verteporfin in DMSO for 24 or 48 h. Supernatants were collected and stored at −80 °C until use.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec18">AT2 cell Immunofluorescence staining</h3><p>AT2 cells were seeded on poly-l-lysine treated coverslips. Cells were stopped at day 2 or after 48 h of respective treatment and fixed with ice-cold acetone-methanol (1:1) for 10 min and washed 3 times with 0.1% BSA in PBS. Next, cells were permeabilized with 0.1% Triton X-100 solution in PBS for 20 min, blocked with 5% BSA in PBS for 30 min at room temperature and incubated with primary antibodies (see Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">4</a>) followed by secondary antibodies, 1 h each. DAPI (Roche, Basel, Switzerland) staining for 10 min was used to visualize cell nuclei. Next, coverslips were fixed with 4% PFA for 10 min, mounted with fluorescent mounting medium (Dako, Glostrup,</p><p>Denmark) and visualized with an Axio Imager M2 Microscope (Zeiss) or confocal microscope (LSM 710; Zeiss).</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec19">RNA isolation and reverse transcription quantitative polymerase chain reaction (RT-qPCR)</h3><p>Standard nucleic acid extraction protocols were used in obtaining RNA from monolayered cells as previously described using peqGold Total RNA kit (Peqlab, Erlangen, Germany)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 97" title="Ulke, H. M. et al. The oncogene ECT2 contributes to a hyperplastic, proliferative lung epithelial cell phenotype in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 61, 713–726 (2019)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR97" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3167">97</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 103" title="Mutze, K., Vierkotten, S., Milosevic, J., Eickelberg, O. &amp; Königshoff, M. Enolase 1 (ENO1) and protein disulfide-isomerase associated 3 (PDIA3) regulate Wnt/β-catenin-driven trans-differentiation of murine alveolar epithelial cells. Dis. Model Mech. 8, 877–890 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR103" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3170">103</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 104" title="Mümmler, C. et al. Cell-specific expression of runt-related transcription factor 2 contributes to pulmonary fibrosis. FASEB J. 32, 703–716 (2018)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR104" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3173">104</a></sup>.</p><p>Lung lobes extracted immediately following termination of all in vivo experiments were snapfrozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C until further processing. RNA was isolated by mechanically homogenizing tissue using the Mikro Dismembrator S (Sartorius Group, Germany). RNA isolation from pulverized mouse lung tissue was performed using TRIZol followed by purification using peqGOLD columns.</p><p>RNA isolation from human PCLS was done using RNeasy Fibrous Tissue Mini Kit (Qiagen, Germany) and Quick RNA Microprep kit (R1051). RNeasy Fibrous Tissue Mini Kit (Qiagen, Germany) was used with minor modifications. Pulverized human PCLS were incubated in RLT lysis buffer containing 4 mM Dithiothreitol (DTT) for 10 min on ice. Proteinase K was added and incubated at room temperature for 15 min. Lysates were passed through DNA removal columns and loaded on RNeasy mini columns and processed further per the manufacturer’s instructions. For Quick RNA Microprep kit (R1051), after homogenization of the hPCLS in lysis buffer we followed the manufacturer’s instructions.</p><p>RNA concentrations and purity were assessed using NanoDrop 1000 (Thermo Fischer Scientific, Germany). cDNA synthesis was prepared using SuperScriptTM II (Invitrogen, Carlsbad, CA, USA). qPCR reactions were performed with the primers (Eurofin Genomics and IDT) listed in Supplementary Tables <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">2</a>, <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">3</a> and SYBR green Master Mix (Roche, Germany). Primers were used at a final concentration of 500 nM and qPCR reactions were performed on an LC light cycler 480 (Roche). Δ Cp = Cp(HPRT) – Cp (gene).</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec20">Western blotting</h3><p>Protein lysis in most experiments was done using a modified RIPA buffer containing 50 mM Tris-HCl, 150 mM NaCl, 1 mM egtazic acid (EGTA), 1 mM ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA), 1% NP-40, 2.5 mM Tetrasodium pyrophosphate, and 1% sodium deoxycholate (SDC). Mouse lung lobes, mouse PCLS, and human PCLS were snap frozen in liquid nitrogen and pulverized using mikro dismembrator S (Sartorius Group, Germany). Samples were then lysed in RIPA buffer for 30 min on ice and centrifugated at 15,000 × <i>g</i> for 20 min. Protein lysis of monolayered cell cultures was done by directly applying RIPA lysis buffer and shaking on ice for 15 min. Supernatant samples and bronchioalveolar lavage (BAL) were concentrated by freeze-drying and reconstitution in lysis buffer at a 10X concentration. Samples were denatured in Laemelli buffer at 95 °C with or without beta-mercaptoethanol. Gel electrophoresis was done using Tris based gels at ranging concentrations (6–15%) with the addition of 2,2,2-trichloroethanol (TCE) to enable Stain-Free visualization all protein lanes of the gel (Bio-Rad). Prior to transfer, gels were activated using UV for 45 s to detect full protein lanes using fluorescence detection. PVDF and nitrocellulose membranes were used for blotting and blocking was done using 5% non-fat dried milk in Tris buffered saline – Tween 20 (TBST). Primary antibodies were incubated at 4 °C overnight and secondary antibodies were incubated at room temperature for 1 h unless stated otherwise. Protein bands were visualized using Biorad’s ChemiDOC XPS (Biorad) and quantified using ImageLab v6.0 (Biorad)</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec21">In vitro cell-free collagen gelation assay</h3><p>Primary cell culture supernatants were used to study their ability to alter collagen assembly. All samples and their controls were collected in equal volumes, freeze-dried and reconstituted in the same volume of carbonate buffer as previously described<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 105" title="Knüppel, L. et al. A novel antifibrotic mechanism of nintedanib and pirfenidone. Inhibition of collagen fibril assembly. Am. J. Respir. Cell Mol. Biol. 57, 77–90 (2017)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR105" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3212">105</a></sup>. Rat-tail type I collagen (Corning) was diluted in carbonate buffer at 1 mg/ml, pH 7.8 and kept on ice. The biological fluid was then added and immediately mixed well manually. Samples were then incubated at 37 °C and monitored with optical density measurements for the first two hours at 350 nm (Cytation5, Gen5 Software). Samples were then incubated for a further 48 h to allow gelation to fully complete and the resulting gel was analyzed by immunoblotting or fixed in glutaraldehyde/formaldehyde for analysis with a Jeol JSM-7800F scanning electron microscope (SEM) as previously described<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="De Santis, M. M. et al. Extracellular-matrix-reinforced bioinks for 3D bioprinting human tissue. Adv. Mater. 33, e2005476 (2021)." href="#ref-CR106" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3216">106</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Staab-Weijnitz, C. A. et al. FK506-binding protein 10, a potential novel drug target for idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Am. J. Respir. Crit. Care Med. 192, 455–467 (2015)." href="#ref-CR107" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3216_1">107</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 108" title="Kadler, K. E., Holmes, D. F., Trotter, J. A. &amp; Chapman, J. A. Collagen fibril formation. Biochem. J. 316, 1–11 (1996)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR108" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3219">108</a></sup>. Images were analyzed using the TWOMBLI v1 tool for lacunarity and fractal dimensions<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 109" title="Wershof, E. et al. A FIJI macro for quantifying pattern in extracellular matrix. Life Sci. Alliance 4, e202000880 (2021)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR109" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3223">109</a></sup>.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec22">Microarray analysis</h3><p>RNA quality control was performed using High Sensitivity RNA ScreenTape for an Agilent TapeStation. All samples had RIN &gt; 9.0. Transcriptomic analysis was performed using Clariom™ D mouse microarrays. The expression data were processed using the R package oligo(1) in Bioconductor (<a href="http://www.bioconductor.org/">http://www.bioconductor.org/</a>), including background correction, quantile normalization, log2 transformed and final probe summarization. Using Bioconductor package limma<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 110" title="Ritchie, M. E. et al. limma powers differential expression analyses for RNA-sequencing and microarray studies. Nucleic Acids Res. 43, e47 (2015)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR110" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3242">110</a></sup>, the gene expression of each comparison were compared using a linear model fit followed by Empirical Bayes statistical tests<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 111" title="Carvalho, B. S. &amp; Irizarry, R. A. A framework for oligonucleotide microarray preprocessing. Bioinformatics 26, 2363–2367 (2010)." href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#ref-CR111" id="ref-link-section-d412139209e3246">111</a></sup>. The results are reported with Log2FC and adjusted <i>P</i>-value using Benjamini–Hochberg multiple-test adjustment method implemented in limma package. All bioinformatic analyses were performed using R. Principle component analysis (PCA) was performed on the essential R function <i>princomp</i>. Graphing packages used included <i>ggplot2</i>, <i>plotly</i>, and <i>pheatmap</i>. Matrix design was performed in a paired manner between the different siRNA treatments on AT2 cells. Contrast matrix included comparisons between all conditions. Gene lists were produced using a cut off <i>p</i>-value of 0.05.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec23">Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy (FTIR) data collection and processing</h3><p>5 um lung tissue slices from paraffin-embedded samples were analyzed in transmission mode using a Diamond-ATR accessory for a Cary630 FTIR with KBr optics (Agilent). Spectra were collected from 4000 to 650 cm<sup>−1</sup> at a resolution of 2 cm<sup>−1</sup> with 8 measurements taken as background and 8 measurements per sample and averaged. Absorbance was calculated using the formula:</p><p>Absorbance = 2 − log(%<i>T</i> ) where <i>T</i> is transmittance.</p><p>Paraffin spectra were collected and subtracted from all samples. Spectra were smoothened using a Savitzky–Golay filter with order 3 and length 51 prior to background subtraction in R. Quasar version 1.10.2, an open-source toolbox extending the capabilities of Orange 3.37.0 and Orange Spectroscopy v.0.7.2, was used for second derivative spectroscopy to confirm peak identification according to the literature (Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">3</a>) prior to extraction of background corrected samples for statistical comparison.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec24">Sample preparation and nuclei extraction from human PCLS</h3><p>Four PCLS slices per control donor (<i>n </i>= 2) at day 5 after DMSO, FC, FC+Verteporfin stimulation were washed in cold 1X PBS and snap frozen. Nuclei were extracted using the Nuclei Isolation kit (CG000505, 10X Genomics,). Briefly and based on the manufacturer’s protocol and reagents, the tissue was dissociated on ice, centrifugated and washed. The pellet was resuspended, and cellular debris was removed. Following another centrifugation step, nuclei were resuspended and counted.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec25">Single-cell barcoding, library preparation, and sequencing</h3><p>Around 20’000 nuclei were loaded on a Chip G with Chromium Single Cell 3′ v3.1 gel beads and reagents (3′ GEX v3.1, 10x Genomics). Final libraries were analyzed on an Agilent Bioanalyzer High Sensitivity DNA chip for qualitative control purposes. cDNA libraries were sequenced on a HiSeq 4000 Illumina platform aiming for 150 million reads per library and a sequencing configuration of 26 base pair (bp) on read1 and 98 bp on read2.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec26">Fastq generation and read trimming</h3><p>Basecalls were converted to reads with the software Cell Ranger’s (v4.0.0) implementation mkfastq. Multiple fastq files from the same library and strand were catenated to single files. Read2 files were subject to two passes of contaminant trimming with cutadapt (i) for the template switch oligo sequence (AAGCAGTGGTATCAACGCAGAGTACATGGG) anchored on the 5′ end and (ii) for poly(A) sequences on the 3′ end. Following trimming, read pairs were removed if the read2 was trimmed below 30 bp.</p><p>Paired reads were filtered if either the cell barcode or unique molecular identifier (UMI) sequence had more than 1 bp with a phred of &lt;20. Reads were aligned with STAR (v2.7.9a) to the human genome reference GRCh38 release 99 from ensemble. Collapsed UMIs with reads that span both exonic and intronic sequences were retained as both separate and combined gene expression assays.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec27">Filtering cell barcodes and quality control</h3><p>After preprocessing, analysis of the ex vivo human PCLS snRNA-seq data was conducted using the Seurat package (version 4.1.3). Nucleii with less than 750 transcripts profiled and with a percentage of reads that map to the mitochondrial genome &gt;3% were then removed. The average number of nuclei returned per library is 10,281 (±6517). A detailed summary of the average UMI and gene per cell type per library distribution per treatment condition is presented in Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s41467-025-61795-x#MOESM1">S10</a>.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec28">Integration and analysis</h3><p>To minimize the possible effect of potential batch correction methods, we first processed and annotated each library separately, before integrating them together and annotating them jointly. To integrate the multiple snRNA-seq datasets, we employed Robust Principal Component Analysis (RPCA). RPCA is a powerful technique for decomposing a data matrix into low-rank and sparse components.
